Reading list

A personal arXiv digest · Mauro Bernardi

Last successful feed update: 2026-11-20T16:24:32+00:00. The date on a card is its first appearance in this reader, not necessarily its original arXiv submission date.

The reader monitors stat.ME, stat.CO, stat.AP and ranks titles and abstracts against my research interests. Scores express relevance to this profile, not scientific quality or a probability of correctness.

For foundational articles and books, see Selected readings in statistics.

How papers are selected

This reading list reflects my interests in quantile and distributional regression, variational inference and Bayesian computation, sparsity and model selection, functional data analysis, statistical networks, high-dimensional covariance estimation, hierarchical and dynamic models, and optimisation methods such as ADMM, MM and gradient-based algorithms.

Papers are ranked by matching their titles and abstracts to these themes. Contributions by the authors listed below receive an additional relevance signal, but authorship is neither a requirement for inclusion nor a measure of scientific quality.

Authors followed

Roger Koenker, Victor Chernozhukov, Xuming He, Cristian Castiglione, Nicolas Bianco, Daniele Bianchi, Antonio Canale, Marco Stefanucci, Monia Lupparelli, Alberto Roverato, Manuela Cattelan, Claudio Busatto, Lea Petrella, Roberto Casarin, Luca Maestrini, Matt Wand, James Ramsay, Piotr Kokoszka, Jane-Ling Wang, Fang Yao, Håvard Rue, Leonhard Held, Simon Wood, David Blei, David Dunson, Andrew Gelman, Sylvia Frühwirth-Schnatter, Arnaud Doucet, Nicolas Chopin, Christian Robert, Robert Tibshirani, Ryan Tibshirani, Trevor Hastie, Bradley Efron, Peter Bühlmann, Martin Wainwright, Emmanuel Candès, Kenneth Lange, Stephen Boyd, Lieven Vandenberghe, Neal Parikh, Dimitri Bertsekas, Yurii Nesterov, Amir Beck.

From collection to selection

The shortlist presents up to 20 recent papers with a relevance score of at least 4.0/10. All relevant recent papers are available separately, while the complete recent collection also includes papers below this threshold. Recent records remain available for 7 days from their first collection.

The thematic library retains relevant papers beyond this window and grows through topic-based historical searches. Its coverage is bounded by the search queries and is not exhaustive of arXiv. Scores indicate relevance to my interests, rather than scientific quality; each paper identifies the ranking method used.

Shortlist

The 20 highest-ranked relevant papers from the recent collection, or fewer when the threshold is not met.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Additive Fr\'echet Regression for Random Objects

Changwon Choi, Wookyeong Song, Hans-Georg M\"uller, Byeong U. Park

Networks and covariance estimationHierarchical and mixed models

Relevance 4.0/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Regression analysis for complex data taking values in a general metric space has gained increasing attention in recent years, particularly in the context of Fr\'echet regression with Euclidean predictors $\mathbb{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{p}$. However, local Fr\'echet regression, while more flexible than global Fr\'echet regression, suffers from the curse of dimensionality for the case of multivariate predictors. To address this issue while maintaining model flexibility, we introduce a framework of additive structured nonparametric regression when responses are situated in a general metric space. Overcoming the challenge of the lack of vector space structure in general metric spaces where the responses reside requires a novel approach that incorporates the additive structure via projection operators. This leads to a unified framework of additive regression for a wide range of response types, including one-dimensional distributions in Wasserstein space, network data represented by graph Laplacians and spherical data equipped with geodesic distances, among others. We establish pointwise and uniform consistency, including convergence rates, for the proposed additive Fr\'echet regression estimators, using smooth backfitting. The practical utility of this additive model is demonstrated through applications to brain connectivity network analysis using resting-state fMRI data and distributional physical activity data.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Infinity-Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Principal Component Analysis: Exact Reformulation, Finite-Sample Guarantee, and Efficient Algorithm

Zhouxing Luo, Lei Wang, Xin Liu

Networks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.0/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Principal component analysis (PCA) is a fundamental and ubiquitous technique for dimension reduction and representation learning, yet its empirical formulation remains vulnerable to distributional uncertainty. While distributionally robust optimization (DRO) offers a natural remedy, existing order-2 Wasserstein models of PCA either preserve the nominal PCA subspace or lack an exact reformulation. This paper demonstrates how the infinity-Wasserstein distance can reconcile these two desirable properties. For the resulting DRO model of PCA, we derive an exact finite-dimensional reformulation and explicitly identify a worst-case distribution attaining the inner maximum. Crucially, the reformulated objective retains samplewise residual information beyond the empirical covariance matrix, revealing a mechanism through which distributional robustness can reshape the learned principal subspace. Under the mild assumption that the true distribution has a finite fourth moment, we further establish a uniform finite-sample performance guarantee for the proposed model. An iteratively reweighted majorization-minimization algorithm is also developed to solve the resulting nonconvex and nonsmooth optimization problem, with a rigorous analysis of its convergence properties. Numerical experiments substantiate the computational efficiency of the proposed algorithm and the practical effectiveness of the infinity-Wasserstein model in downstream applications.

Infinity-Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Principal Component Analysis: Exact Reformulation, Finite-Sample Guarantee, and Efficient Algorithm

Networks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.0/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Infinity-Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Principal Component Analysis: Exact Reformulation, Finite-Sample Guarantee, and Efficient Algorithm

Zhouxing Luo, Lei Wang, Xin Liu

Networks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.0/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Exact Bayesian layers via characteristic functions

William H. W. Thompson, Guillaume St-Onge, Laurent H\'ebert-Dufresne, Jean-Gabriel Young

Variational inference and Bayesian computation

Relevance 2.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Bayesian neural networks provide principled uncertainty estimates, but training them with Monte Carlo sampling can be expensive. Sampling-free variational inference algorithms make training Bayesian neural networks feasible, but existing approaches assume a Gaussian output distribution, which can be inaccurate in applied problems. Here, we introduce CF-Prop, an uncertainty propagation method that uses characteristic functions to compute the exact output distribution of a Bayesian ReLU layer, without any sampling, even in non-Gaussian settings. Because CF-Prop propagates characteristic functions, the evidence lower bound---the objective of variational training---can be computed in closed form for a broad class of likelihoods, including the Gaussian, Poisson, and Gamma. This allows to implement an efficient resulting variational inference algorithm, CF-VI. On CIFAR-100, CF-VI achieves the lowest calibration error of any tested method, improving on the next best method by about 35%. On a heavy-tailed insurance claim dataset, CF-VI accurately captures tail uncertainty that is severely underestimated by both other sampling-free methods and Monte Carlo methods.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Sensor Monitoring Towards Industry 4.0: Bridging Concepts on the $C_n$ Multivariate Control Chart

Diego C. Nascimento, Ana C. S. Batista, Francisco Louzada, Paulo H. Ferreira

Sparsity and model selection

Relevance 2.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The main challenge in monitoring multiple sensors is the high dimensionality that a process can easily reach. From a decision-making perspective, converting these multivariate dynamics into an informative visualization is also not trivial. Statistical Process Control (SPC) techniques detect changes in quality characteristics, but they are difficult to apply beyond the normality (Gaussian) assumption. For instance, the most well-known SPC multivariate control chart is based on Hotelling's $T^2$ statistic, which assumes that the process data follow a multivariate normal distribution (which rarely occurs in practice) and that the observations are serially independent. This study focuses on the multivariate case (dimension $d \geq 2$), regardless of the data dependence or marginal asymmetry. We propose a new control chart for change detection in copula-based multivariate processes, called the $C_n$-chart, which is based on a density level-set tolerance region built from a copula function, and we derive its run-length properties. The methodology also quantifies the (not necessarily linear) dependence among the variables. Two case studies are presented, one on water quality control and the other on monitoring brass-steel thermostats, complemented by a simulation study of run-length performance and computational cost. The results show that the proposed methodology captures both the association among variables and the anomalies, and that it accommodates variable selection through conditional (regression) modeling of the margins.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Inferring Neural Activity Changes under Recording Uncertainty with Transformers and Variational Inference

Nilson Chapagain, Pooja Chapagain

Variational inference and Bayesian computation

Relevance 2.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

A difference in recorded firing rate across days may reflect a change in neuronal activity, missed spikes or recording a different neuron. We present a statistical approach that combines neuron tracking with Bayesian estimates of stationary mean recorded firing rates. Bounds under assumed differences in spike detection connect recorded rates to biological rate changes. We assess recording loss and uncertainty in rate changes in simulations, and count-model fit in recordings. We also compare two transformer estimators with Gaussian and flow variational inference under a shared temporal negative-binomial model. Flow variational inference agrees most closely with reference posteriors, while predictive performance varies across mice. Rate-change intervals contain true simulated changes less often than intended. In UnitMatch recordings from five mice, some inferred changes remain supported when spike detection is allowed to differ between days; others depend on equal detection. These interpretations require a correct neuron match. The approach identifies which inferred rate changes remain supported under specified recording assumptions and where uncertainty limits interpretation.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.AP · stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Bayesian Computation of Copula-based Multivariate Models with an Application to Doping Detection

Emanuele Masillo, Eva Pigi Viltanioti, Nina Deliu, Brunero Liseo

Variational inference and Bayesian computation

Relevance 2.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The Athlete Biological Passport (ABP) monitors athletes for doping by comparing their biomarker readings against personalized reference ranges. These are estimated via the Bayesian ADAPTIVE model, which analyzes biomarkers separately and ignores their dependence. Building on a recently proposed copula-based multivariate extension of ADAPTIVE, this paper focuses on its computational aspects, introducing a fully joint MCMC algorithm and comparing it with a two-step Bayesian IFM estimator. Simulations show the two procedures perform comparably under a correctly specified copula, but exhibit a genuine trade-off under misspecification: the Bayesian IFM protects marginal inference, while the joint MCMC yields better-calibrated dependence estimates and is markedly faster. The two approaches are tested on a dataset containing ABP profiles of elite athletes, provided by NADO Italia, and collected as part of the project {\it Enhancing the methodological framework for doping detection: from univariate to multivariate testing analysis on Athlete Biological Passport profiles}. Both approaches achieve consistent classification performance, providing anti-doping organizations with two concrete inferential routes for adopting a multivariate ADAPTIVE model, along with guidance on the trade-offs between them.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Improving External Prediction in New Clusters with Mixed-Effects Models: Implications for Multicenter Studies

Jiawa Zhang, Hyungrok Do, Myeonggyun Lee

Hierarchical and mixed models

Relevance 2.5/10. Matched topics: Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Prediction models developed from multicenter data are often applied to new centers. A linear mixed model (LMM) captures between-center heterogeneity through random effects. However, a new center's random effect is unknown, so the LMM predicts from fixed effects alone, as does a standard linear model (LM) that ignores clustering. The two models thus differ only in how fixed effects are estimated, and it is unclear whether the LMM predicts better. We decompose the prediction error in a new center into fixed-effect estimation error, between-center variation, and residual variation. Because the last two components are identical for both models, any LMM advantage must come from more precise fixed-effect estimates. In simulations across diverse designs and random-effect structures, the LMM generally achieved lower mean squared error than the LM. Relative gains increased with the number of predictors, between-center heterogeneity, and imbalance in center sizes, and decreased as training data grew. Modeling a random slope yielded additional gains that increased with between-center variation in the predictor effect. In a stress test using held-out eICU hospitals selected for strong outcome heterogeneity, a random-intercept LMM predicted ICU length of stay more accurately than an LM. These findings clarify the conditions under which mixed models can improve prediction in new centers and can inform model development for multicenter studies.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Single-fit inference for the realized prediction error of a logistic model with many predictors

Seyoung Park, Eun Ryung Lee

Sparsity and model selection

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

When independent validation data are unavailable, evaluating a fitted logistic model on new patients requires estimating prediction error and uncertainty. Evaluating the fit on training data is often optimistic. Bootstrap validation is widely used to correct this bias, but with many predictors, it can retain bias and yield unreliable confidence intervals. In this article, we study these limitations and develop single-fit estimation and inference without resampling. When the number of Gaussian predictors grows proportionally with sample size, we analyze unpenalized logistic models. We prove that standard bootstrap correction can have nonzero limiting bias even as the resample count grows and location-shifted intervals can have zero limiting coverage. Instead, we adjust the fitted scores to consistently estimate realized Brier and log losses. We account for common fitting and adjustment error to construct valid marginal intervals and joint regions. We also establish these single-fit estimation and inference guarantees for certain non-Gaussian designs in proportional dimensions. Under moderate dimension growth and possible misspecification with general predictors, we extend single-fit inference to ridge regularization and construct asymptotically valid confidence intervals for realized log loss. Simulations and analyses of hospital and diabetes datasets support our theory and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Norm-equation methods for structured group-penalized regression

Jiayi Yu, Baisuo Jin

Sparsity and model selection

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Group-penalized regression with correlated predictors requires block updates for which familiar group soft thresholding formulas are generally inapplicable. Although proximal-gradient methods can solve such updates directly, their computational cost depends on the penalty and the design. We develop a norm-equation framework for a Euclidean group penalty combined with a finite, closed, convex, positively homogeneous within-group penalty. Each nonzero block minimizer is characterized by a unique scalar root, evaluated through strongly convex inner problems without enumerating sparsity or fusion patterns. Valid inner subgradients provide computable bounds on the scalar function and the block Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) error. These bounds allow scalar-search decisions and acceptance of approximate updates without solving every inner problem to high accuracy. Accounting for gradient changes during a cyclic sweep then gives a computable global KKT bound. Combined with standard stochastic-score and restricted-curvature conditions, this bound leads to a regularization-scaled stopping rule under which approximate estimators retain the usual estimation and prediction rates. Experiments with sparse-group and fused sparse-group regression show that computational performance depends strongly on the inner solver, with coordinate-descent and dual implementations offering advantages over direct proximal methods in some highly correlated settings.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

A Unified Unfolding and Slide-Vector Model for Asymmetric Dissimilarities via Clustered Lasso

Ryohei Moriya, Kensuke Tanioka, Hiroshi Yadohisa

Sparsity and model selection

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Asymmetric multidimensional scaling (AMDS) methods represent asymmetric dissimilarity data in a low-dimensional space. Existing approaches include the highly flexible unfolding model and the parsimonious slide-vector model. However, a highly flexible model is not necessarily optimal for adequately representing asymmetric relationships, and a model with an intermediate level of complexity may be more effective. In this study, we propose a new AMDS model that integrates the unfolding and slide-vector models by incorporating a clustered Lasso penalty. By fusing similar slide vectors, the proposed method can represent intermediate model structures and adjust model complexity according to the observed data. In numerical simulations, we compared the proposed method with existing methods by varying the number of groups, the number of objects, and the number of dimensions. The results demonstrated that the proposed method achieved high estimation accuracy across various asymmetric structures. Furthermore, in an application to cola brand-switching data, the proposed method achieved the highest accuracy in reproducing asymmetric relationships among the three methods compared and yielded a lower stress value than the slide-vector model. These findings suggest that adaptively adjusting model complexity may be effective for representing asymmetric relationships more accurately.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Inference in High-Dimensional Linear Mediation Models

Samriddha Lahiry, Rajarshi Mukherjee, Pragya Sur

Sparsity and model selection

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Mediation analysis plays an important role in psychology, epidemiology, and genomics. Al- though high-dimensional mediation has been studied extensively, existing inferential methods typically rely on sparsity assumptions that may be difficult to justify when many individually weak mediators collectively produce a substantial effect. We study inference for both the over- all indirect effect and the effect transmitted through an individual mediator in a proportional high-dimensional regime, where the number of mediators and the sample size grow at compa- rable rates, without imposing sparsity on the mediator effects. Standard debiasing procedures for high-dimensional inference commonly rely on design-covariance information or additional structural assumptions on precision-matrix directions. In mediation models, however, the full outcome-design covariance depends on unknown mediator-model coefficients, creating an addi- tional challenge when such information or structure is unavailable. To address this challenge, we introduce an approximate debiasing procedure based on partial ridge regression and show that the resulting estimator is asymptotically bias-corrected and Gaussian. We also develop a cross-fitted estimator with a smaller asymptotic variance than its sample-split counterpart. In addition, we construct consistent estimators of the limiting variances of both the sample-split and cross-fitted estimators. Together, these results provide feasible inferential procedures for global and pathwise mediation effects.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Statistical Inference with Ill-Conditioned Random Weight Neural Network Sieves

Giovanni Ballarin, Lyudmila Grigoryeva, Lukas Gonon

Networks and covariance estimation

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Random weight neural networks (RWNNs) are shallow networks whose hidden-layer parameters are randomly generated and then kept fixed, while only the output coefficients are estimated. Conditional on the sampled parameters, RWNN regression is a linear sieve problem with random approximation space. We show that such random sieves cannot remain uniformly well-conditioned as their dimension grows. For parametrized feature families satisfying polynomial covering and Lipschitz conditions, the eigenvalues of the population covariance matrix are proven to have polynomial upper bounds determined by the covering geometry of the parameter space, leading to a vanishing smallest eigenvalue. We develop approximation, estimation, and inference theory that explicitly treats the ill-conditioning. Under a $\beta$-H\"older source condition, we obtain both faster-than-Monte-Carlo $L^2$ approximation rates and new $L^\infty$ approximation bounds based on stratified sampling. We then derive improved consistency guarantees for ridge-regularized least squares driven by ridge effective dimension, spectral, and leverage-based stability conditions. For inference, we establish asymptotic linearity and Gaussian limits for linear functional directions while allowing the smallest eigenvalue of the population covariance matrix to vanish. Nondegeneracy is directional: we characterize it with a ridge-normalized Rayleigh quotient and extend it to smooth functionals via Riesz representation. Consistency of ridge-adjusted variance estimators is proved, yielding feasible studentized inference. Numerical experiments illustrate the deterioration of random-feature conditioning and the finite-sample behavior of the resulting estimation and inference procedures.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Spatiotemporal Risk-Based Hotspot Dynamics Assessment

Jos\'e M. Angulo, Ana E. Madrid

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Analysis of hotspots often constitutes the final objective in application studies oriented to spatiotemporal risk assessment, particularly for risk mapping and as a helpful instrument for decision making, in diverse fields such as Ecology, Epidemiology, Environmental Sciences, Security, among others. The most primary form, common in practice, consists in identifying and depicting the areas where the magnitude of interest exceeds a certain critical threshold level. In this work, we introduce a wider, more general concept of `hotspot', associated with the extremal values of risk measures defined on different structural indicators of threshold exceedance sets, such as the exceedance area, the excess volume, etc. In particular, we propose a methodological approach based on empirical characterization of the indicator probability distributions from simulation conditional on the available observations, calculated at local resolution scales determined by sliding windows. Risk map generation is then supported by a proper conceptualization and formalization of a suitable family of risk conditional random fields (RCRFs), specified in terms of a set of input elements that can be viewed as flexible tuning parameters for practical implementation and application. For illustration, we consider an extension of a spatiotemporal diffusive blur-generated autoregressive model involving a dynamical spatial deformation. Evolutionary assessment of hotspots is then addressed under different scenarios, with reference to well-known quantile-based risk measures.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

BRUCE (Bayesian Rio Urban Climate Estimator): spatiotemporal interpolation of air temperature from heterogeneous sensor networks for operational heat monitoring

Ayrton Sena Gouveia, Rafael Santos de Oliveira, Lucas Menezes-Silva, Maira Alejandra Moreno Castillo, Felipe de Carvalho Vommaro Marincola, Jo\~ao Henrique de Ara\'ujo Morais, Val\'eria Saraceni, Gislani Mateus Oliveira Aguilar, Caroline Dias Ferreira, Oswaldo Gon\c{c}alves Cruz

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Accurate characterization of intra-urban temperature variability is essential for heat-health surveillance, yet official monitoring networks are too sparse to resolve fine-scale gradients, particularly in Global South cities. We present BRUCE, a Bayesian hierarchical spatiotemporal model, fitted with the Integrated Nested Laplace Approximation (INLA) and a Stochastic Partial Differential Equation (SPDE) spatial component, that fuses four heterogeneous temperature sources for Rio de Janeiro, Brazil: official weather stations, low-cost IoT sensors, citizen-science personal weather stations and ERA5-Land reanalysis. The model accounts for class- and station-specific measurement bias and estimates a continuous spatial field that evolves day to day through an autoregressive (AR1) structure. Fitted independently to nine monthly windows (January, June, November; 2023--2025), the elevation coefficient was significant in all windows and of consistent magnitude in eight ($-$1.08 to $-$1.38 \textdegree{}C per SD). In leave-region-out cross-validation against LCZ4r, accuracy was comparable (RMSE differences within station-level bootstrap uncertainty in all three windows), while BRUCE produced predictions at every held-out station, including those where LCZ4r is masked, and preserved finer-scale spatial structure than LCZ4r's discrete zone classification. Applied to 241 primary care territories, BRUCE's posterior exceedance probabilities allowed 96 to be classified with high confidence (model-based classifications), whereas a constant-width interval derived from LCZ4r cross-validation errors supported confident classification of none. The framework offers a statistically principled, operationally viable basis for intra-urban heat monitoring in data-heterogeneous settings.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.AP · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Laplace Bayesian transport maps for generative modeling of non-Gaussian climate fields

Jacob A. Johnson, Felix Jimenez, Matthias Katzfuss

Variational inference and Bayesian computation

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Transport maps provide a powerful framework for the generative modeling of complex, non-Gaussian spatial fields, such as those encountered in climate science. By mapping a complicated target distribution to a standard Gaussian reference distribution, they can serve as efficient stochastic surrogates. However, current Bayesian transport maps, which are modeled using autoregressive Gaussian processes, typically rely on empirical Bayes estimates that ignore hyperparameter uncertainty. Here, we contrast this point-estimate approach with a fully Bayesian model that provides posterior uncertainty quantification in transport map hyperparameters. To avoid the high cost of Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), we also propose a Laplace approximation to the hyperparameter posteriors, which can be computed at negligible additional computational cost relative to empirical Bayes. We provide numerical evidence and a real-data climate application demonstrating that these Laplace approximations can accurately capture most posterior summaries at a fraction of the cost of MCMC, while maintaining good predictive properties. Thus, our approach offers a scalable, uncertainty-aware framework for the generative modeling of non-Gaussian climate fields.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

A Free Lunch: Gradient Averaging Achieves Optimal Statistical Efficiency in Newton Sketch

Haoxuan Wang, Jianliang Ye, Sen Na

Optimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study statistical inference for online sketched Newton methods, in which each Newton system is approximately solved by an inner randomized sketching solver. Existing work reveals a computational--statistical trade-off: the sketching solver (and its accelerated variants) reduces the per-step cost of Newton methods from $O(d^3)$ to $O(d^2)$, but the randomness introduced by sketching also inflates the limiting covariance of both the last and the averaged Newton iterates. In this work, we address this trade-off via gradient averaging. We show that simply replacing the stochastic gradient with a moving average at each step can mitigate, and in some cases eliminate, the trade-off in Newton sketch. Importantly, gradient averaging requires no additional oracle evaluations and leaves the contraction bound of the inner sketching solver unchanged. In particular, we prove that the last sketched Newton iterate with gradient averaging attains a limiting covariance no larger than that without gradient averaging, while the averaged iterate even attains the minimax optimal limiting covariance, matching that of the exact Newton method. To our knowledge, this is the first analysis showing that randomness introduced to improve computational efficiency need not compromise statistical efficiency as long as proper gradient estimates are used. The key analytical challenge is that gradient averaging destroys the crucial martingale difference structure underlying central limit theorem arguments. We overcome this challenge by analyzing the coupled two-timescale recursions governing the iterate and the gradient estimator. Numerical experiments on linear and logistic regression support our theoretical covariance comparisons.

arXiv abstract · PDF
stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

WAIVES: Weak-form Asymptotically-unbiased Instrumental-Variable Estimation for SDEs

Hamza Ruzayqat, David M. Bortz, Vanja Duki\'c

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Weak-form methods have transformed inference for deterministic dynamical systems. By integrating the governing equations against smooth, compactly supported test functions, they trade noise-amplifying differentiation for noise-attenuating integration, yielding fast and robust estimates. Extending this idea to stochastic differential equations (SDEs) has proven harder, and a consistent single-trajectory formulation has remained out of reach. Applied naively in the time domain, the weak form makes the regressors and the errors functionals of the same noise, creating endogeneity that leaves weak-form least squares biased and inconsistent. We resolve it with instrumental variables, a classical idea from econometrics. Weak-form integrals over nearby disjoint time windows prove to be valid instruments, correlated with the regressors yet uncorrelated with the error, restoring consistency and asymptotic normality. The resulting generalized-method-of-moments estimator, WAIVES (Weak-form Asymptotically-unbiased Instrumental-Variable Estimation for SDEs), requires only a linear solve, with no likelihood evaluations, filtering, or simulation. It is therefore an order of magnitude faster than exact-likelihood methods where those exist, and it applies unchanged where they do not, with standard errors and calibrated confidence intervals supplied by the moment conditions. It also separates measurement noise from the intrinsic randomness of the dynamics. Across nine example systems, spanning linear, bistable, chaotic, excitable, state-dependent-diffusion, non-polynomial, hypoelliptic, and high-dimensional regimes (up to 120 dimensions), WAIVES recovers nonlinear drift and diffusion with negligible bias and near-nominal interval coverage. We close with two real-data illustrations: a 167-year North Atlantic sea-surface-temperature record and an optical-tweezer calibration.

stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Global sensitivity analysis of transient regimes in non-Newtonian dam-break flows applied in rheometry

Yuri Taglieri S\'ao, Jo\~ao Batista Pereira, Geraldo de Freitas Maciel

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Non-Newtonian dam-break flows are a classical problem with numerous engineering applications. These flows are particularly important in alternative rheometry, where they are used to rapidly and affordably estimate rheological properties, such as in the slump test and Bostwick consistometer test. However, the lifting dynamics of the containing obstacle introduce inertial effects that can compromise the accurate estimation of these properties. These effects are influenced by the lifting velocity, the fluid's viscosity, and the initial geometry of the sample. This study aims to quantify the influence of lifting velocity and to provide physical insights into how inertial effects influence the flow. A global sensitivity analysis (GSA) framework adapted for functional outputs (e.g., time series) is employed, using data generated from high-fidelity simulations with ANSYS Fluent. The GSA produces sensitivity curves that track the evolving importance of each input parameter over time. Three distinct flow regimes are identified, namely mobilization, transition and viscous regimes, each governed by different dominant factors. The first two regimes are significantly affected by the lifting velocity, revealing the influence of inertial effects, whereas the viscous regime is dominated by fluid rheology and shows a increasing importance of the yield stress. These results are contextualized in terms of traditional rheometry tests.

A Generative Model for Higher-Order Network Dynamics

Valentina S\'anchez, Theodore Papamarkou, Gonzalo N\'apoles, \c{C}i\c{c}ek G\"uven, Koen V. Haak

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Conventional network representations inferred from multivariate time series describe statistical relationships between pairs of components, even when the underlying dynamics involve group interactions that cannot be represented by pairwise interactions alone. Several multivariate information-theoretic measures have been proposed to characterise dependence beyond pairs. However, due to a lack of systems with known generating mechanisms, these measures have not been carefully validated or compared for empirical use. To address this, we here introduce a generative model of stochastic network dynamics with pairwise and higher-order interactions specified by the user. The higher-order interactions are represented by directed hyperedges, and the model provides a controlled benchmark for evaluating information-theoretic measures. Removing only the higher-order term gives a pairwise-only reference. The experiments test whether a measure detects changes induced by the higher-order term relative to this reference and recovers the participating nodes and their source-target roles. A separate experiment tests discrimination between a product coupling and a specified nonlinear pairwise control, with equal root-mean-square coupling magnitude under the pairwise-only reference. Several measures detected sufficiently strong higher-order interactions across the tested network topologies and coupling functions. In the separate discrimination experiment, the measures also separated the product coupling from the specified nonlinear pairwise control. Recovery, however, became harder as the number of candidate interactions grew. The framework therefore provides a testbed where the link between higher-order signatures and mechanisms can be tested, rather than assumed, before measures are applied to empirical data.

Scalable Non-Parametric Bayesian Inference of Free Energy Landscapes and Emission Functions from Observations

Eliane Briand, Helmut Grubm\"uller

Dynamic and spatial models

Relevance 2.0/10. Matched topics: Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Free-energy landscapes govern processes from protein conformational change to climate oscillations, but are typically observed only indirectly, through observables such as FRET intensity or optical-tweezer transmittance. Neither the landscape shape, nor the emission function mapping latent state to signal, is usually known, and distinct states may produce the same signal. We therefore infer landscape and emission function jointly and non-parametrically, using the exact non-linear Bayesian filtering likelihood, to fully use the information contained in observable trajectories that are often noisy, scarce and costly to acquire. We demonstrate the method on a single-protein optical tweezer recording, where distinct conformations produce the same signal, and beyond biology on the El Nino-Southern Oscillation and financial market volatility.

stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Geometry, Local Minimax Risk, and the Limits of Sample Average Approximation in Linear Stochastic Optimization

Hanbo Yang, Ming Yuan

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study how the local geometry of a linear stochastic optimization problem determines its statistical difficulty, measured by expected decision regret. The optimal local rate is governed by how fast the support function of the feasible set grows away from its linearization at the true mean; depending on the instance, it can be $n^{-1/2}$, $n^{-1}$, $n^{-2}$, or a non-power rate, and sample average approximation (SAA) attains these rates in a Gaussian mean model. At smooth instances, SAA also attains the classical sharp local minimax constant. We then allow the data distribution to vary in a Kullback--Leibler (KL) ball of radius proportional to $1/n$ around a reference distribution $P$. The exact local minimax limit of this benchmark, which may depend on $P$, coincides with that of a Gaussian model whose local parameter set is the covariance ellipsoid of $P$. A simple coordinatewise truncated SAA rule, which does not use $P$, attains the minimax rate, and at smooth instances its constant matches the minimax constant as the neighborhood radius grows. Ordinary SAA, in contrast, can have strictly suboptimal worst-case expected regret when the local rate is fast relative to the tails of $P$, because rare tail events then dominate the expectation. The results delineate when SAA is sharply optimal, rate-optimal, or statistically suboptimal, and quantify the local price of distributional robustness.

Fast and Memory-Light Evaluation of Density-Based Clusterings with the DISCO Index in R

Amin Entezari

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

DISCO is a recent internal cluster validity index for density-based clustering that, unlike earlier indices, also evaluates the quality of noise labels. The discoCVI package makes DISCO available in R. This article describes the design of the package and a typical workflow, in which DISCO is used to compare clusterings and to choose the parameters of HDBSCAN without ground truth, followed by the implementation introduced in version 0.2.0. DISCO is defined through density-connectivity distances between all pairs of points, so direct implementations store several n-by-n matrices. We show that every quantity DISCO needs can be read off the single-linkage merge tree of the mutual-reachability minimum spanning tree, so that the index can be computed exactly with memory that grows linearly with the number of points, while the running time remains quadratic as in the original definition. The implementation is tested against the previous version, agrees with the original Python implementation to within $3 \times 10^{-13}$, and corrects the score of singleton clusters to match the published definition. In benchmarks, discoCVI is 23 to 138 times faster than its previous version for 500 to 4,000 points and 3 to 9 times faster than the Python reference for 500 to 8,000 points, using a fraction of the memory of both, and it scores 100,000 points in about 50 seconds with less than 200 MB of memory.

Jared N. Lakhani

We derive explicit expressions for the mixed moments of the scaled Dirichlet distribution in terms of the Lauricella hypergeometric function of type $D$. Bayesian Nonparametric Survival Regression with Time-Varying Covariates and Random Effects

Kemin Zhang, Yudan Zou, Fan Li, Liangyuan Hu

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In longitudinal survival studies, biomarkers and treatments change across visits, their associations with event risk may be nonlinear and change over time, and subjects with identical measurements may differ in risk. Existing tree-ensemble survival models address these features separately: discrete-time formulations accommodate covariate updates without subject-specific random effects, and hazard-based ensembles with frailty take covariates as fixed at baseline. We propose Time-Varying Survival Bayesian Additive Regression Trees (TVSurvBART), a continuous-time hazard model combining a flexible baseline, a log-linear tree ensemble, and subject-specific random intercepts and slopes; covariates enter as predictable step processes, and interval-start time among the tree inputs lets effects change across visits. Integrated exposure offsets absorb the within-interval variation of the baseline and random slopes, so the tree leaves retain conjugate updates. Re-centering moves along likelihood-invariant directions and alternating centered and non-centered covariance updates address the resulting posterior dependence. In two simulation families, TVSurvBART's landmark predictions from held-out subjects' histories up to the landmark time have higher time-dependent AUC than the Cox, frailty Cox, and forest benchmarks in every setting and lower Brier scores in all but one comparison, although population-summary gains are not uniform. In the CARRA juvenile idiopathic arthritis registry, discrimination for recorded flares is similar to Cox regression, while a secondary interval-probability score favors TVSurvBART.

Fractional Order or False Memory: Identifiability of Caputo SIR Models from Incidence Data

Md Babul Hossain, Mohammad Shah Alam

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

When a fractional order improves the fit of an epidemic model, it is usually taken as evidence of memory. We ask whether the order of the Caputo susceptible-infectious-removed (SIR) model can actually be recovered from incidence data. If the transmission rate is allowed to vary freely in time, as in hybrid forecasting models, every admissible order, removal rate and initial count reproduces the data exactly. With constant transmission and exact data the order is identifiable in principle, but the dominant term of early linearized growth depends on only two parameter combinations: the growth rate r = (beta - gamma)^(1/alpha) and the amplitude beta*I0/alpha. What is left is transient, and it is limited. We prove that parameter sets sharing these two combinations give Poisson count distributions whose Kullback-Leibler divergence stays bounded no matter how long the linear phase is observed, and that the nonlinear model inherits this bound as the population grows. In simulations, wrong orders were rejected in at most 5% of replicates when the data ended two weeks or more before the epidemic peak, and in 95-100% once the peak was included; separation began only a few days before the peak. With mild overdispersion the order was identified only once about 60% of the population had been infected, and with stronger overdispersion not even then. Fits that the early data could not tell apart predicted peaks that differed by 75%, and plug-in forecasts that ignored parameter uncertainty were badly overconfident. Data generated without any memory produced fitted orders below one when transmission changed and when the fitted model was discretized in daily steps. In early COVID-19 data from Bangladesh and the United States, orders from 0.6 to 1 gave almost the same growth rate, whatever value we assumed for the removal rate.

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper presents HAVAPlot, a browser-based framework for scientific visualization and post-processing of numerical simulation data. Operating entirely within the user's browser, HAVAPlot requires no software installation or data uploads while supporting mesh-based and tabular datasets beyond computational fluid dynamics. Meshes containing 250,000 cells load in at most 1.3 seconds. Applications to an impinging supersonic jet, hypersonic cylinder flow, a hybrid particle-continuum cold-spray nozzle, a three-dimensional gas-bearing film, and supersonic cone flow demonstrate the framework's capabilities.

Abstract and links

Synthetic data has emerged as a powerful tool for data sharing, clinical trial simulation, and digital twin development, with growing applications in biomedical research and drug development. Differentially Private Score Estimation with Applications to Score-Based Generative Models

Mengxiao Gao, Elly K. H. Hung, Yuchen Wu, Yi Yu

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Estimating the score function of a distribution plays a central role in statistics and machine learning, with applications in distribution estimation, sampling, and score-based generative modeling. However, learning scores from sensitive data raises privacy concerns. These concerns are particularly pronounced in score-based generative modeling, where trained models may memorize individual training examples and expose sensitive information through membership inference and data extraction attacks. Although non-private score estimation has been extensively studied, the statistical limits of its private counterpart remain less understood. In this work, we study score estimation under $(\alpha,\beta)$-differential privacy for $d$-dimensional sub-Gaussian distributions with locally $\gamma$-H\"older-continuous scores. We develop a sampling-based private score estimator using quadratic B-splines and the exponential mechanism, and we implement our proposal through projected Langevin Monte Carlo with runtime polynomial in the sample size $n$. The proposed estimator satisfies $(\alpha,\beta)$-differential privacy, and achieves expected density-weighted squared error $\mbox{polylog}(n) \max(n^{-2\gamma/(d+2\gamma+2)},\,(n\alpha)^{-2\gamma/(d+\gamma+1)})$. We also establish a nearly matching private minimax lower bound, thereby characterizing the privacy-accuracy trade-off for score estimation. As an application, we construct a differentially private diffusion model whose privacy guarantee is independent of the number of denoising steps. We show that, with an appropriate discretization scheme, the resulting diffusion model attains nearly minimax optimal expected total variation error among all private distribution estimators. We validate our theoretical findings through experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

Adaptive Tests of Pricing Restrictions with Observed and Latent Factors

Anlin Liu, Guowei Yan, Zhaojun Wang

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper develops a high-dimensional test for asset pricing models with observed and latent factors. The null hypothesis allows the intercepts from an observed-factor regression to be nonzero, provided that they lie in the latent factor loading space. We estimate the pricing errors in three steps: regress returns on the observed factors, extract latent factors from the lagged covariance structure of the regression residuals, and regress returns on both observed and estimated latent factors. The intercept from the final regression serves as the estimated pricing error. We construct Sum and Max tests based on the estimated pricing errors to detect diffuse and concentrated alternatives, respectively, and combine them using a Cauchy procedure. Under suitable regularity conditions, we establish the joint null distribution of the component statistics, their asymptotic independence, and their power against alternative hypotheses. We also propose a residual bootstrap and establish its first-order validity. Simulation studies examine the finite-sample performance of the tests under different pricing-error patterns and factor-number choices. An application to S\&P 500 stocks illustrates the use of the proposed framework in evaluating an observed-factor pricing model after latent-factor adjustment.

Dingzi Guo, Wenhui Li, Yalin Wang, Wang Zhou

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Sliced inverse regression is widely used for sufficient dimension reduction, but existing matrix extensions that rely on covariance-based statistics can be unstable under heavy-tailed distributions. We propose matrix elliptical sliced inverse regression (MESIR) for estimating response-relevant row and column reduction subspaces under matrix elliptical distributions. We establish a mode-wise linearity relation linking the inverse regression curve to these subspaces through the corresponding scatter matrices. MESIR combines inverse-regression Kendall's tau matrices with eigenvalue-calibrated marginal Kendall's tau matrices. The calibration recovers the row and column scatter matrices needed to identify the reduction subspaces. Theoretically, we derive convergence rates for the calibrated marginal and inverse-regression estimators and establish subspace consistency under weak moment conditions, allowing the matrix dimensions and number of slices to diverge with the sample size. We further develop a consistent eigenvalue-ratio procedure for structural dimension selection and extend the method and theory to higher-order tensor predictors. Thorough simulation studies are conducted to show competitive performance under light tails and increasingly pronounced gains as the tails become heavier. Applications to COVID-19 CT images and radar target recognition demonstrate favorable empirical performance.

On the trajectories of the ID algorithm

Ivano Lodato, Simran Hiranandani, Aditya Varna Iyer

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The ID algorithm decides whether an interventional distribution can be computed from observational data and, when it can, returns a formula in which products, ratios and summations of conditional probabilities are nested inside one another. We study these formulas by following the trajectories of the calls of the algorithm, each of which ends with a tail (an alternation of lines 7 and 2) and an exit, and we derive two main results. First, line~4 and the exit lines fix the single outer summation of the output, while the tail forms everything inside it, both the inner summations and the ratios. Second, a ratio keeps a summation in its denominator only when a repetition of the tail is entangled, and a test of ancestry in the original graph decides, before any formula is computed, whether a topological order can be chosen so as to avoid it. We also derive a bound on the nesting depth of the output, and show a simple ``dimensional'' count that every output has to satisfy. Together, these results constrain the form of an ID output and guide its implementation and simplification.

General Methodology for Asymptotically Balanced Multiple Tests

Merle Munko

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In statistical applications, often more than one hypothesis is of interest and, thus, multiple testing problems occur naturally. To control the family-wise error rate, popular adjustment methods, like the Bonferroni and Holm correction, have been developed. However, these methods do not use the dependence structure of the test statistics. Consequently, they are conservative in most settings. To increase the power of the tests, the joint dependence structure of the test statistics can be incorporated for the construction of critical values, for example, by using resampling schemes as bootstrap procedures to approximate the joint distribution. This has already been applied in several contexts. In this work, we develop a very flexible and general theoretical foundation, which can be used to construct asymptotically balanced multiple tests that control the family-wise error rate in the strong sense. Additionally, we prove in the general setting that consistent resampling schemes satisfy the needed conditions.

Do Network Mechanisms in Science Vary Across Research Topics? Contextual Relational Hyperevent Models for Large-Scale Bibliometric Data

J\"urgen Lerner, Mark Wittek, Raphael Heiberger

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Collaboration and citation networks in science are shaped by well-documented mechanisms such as preferential attachment, gender homophily, and self-citation. Whether these mechanisms operate uniformly across research areas, however, is rarely tested, because statistical network models that estimate several mechanisms jointly are difficult to apply to large bibliometric data and to compare across subnetworks. We introduce contextual relational hyperevent models (C-RHEMs), which extend relational hyperevent models by allowing network effects to vary across research topics within a single model. C-RHEMs treat each publication as one event linking its authors to its references, accommodate reference lists spanning several topics, and scale to hundreds of thousands of publications on standard hardware. In a second step, meta-analysis and meta-regression quantify heterogeneity in topic-specific effects and relate it to topic characteristics. We illustrate the approach with about 590,000 oncology publications from the PubMed Knowledge Graph, assigned to 32 OpenAlex research topics. We find that preferential attachment, gender homophily, and self-citation vary substantially across topics, with between-topic standard deviations of 30 to 70 percent of the corresponding pooled effects. Topic age, size, and gender composition account for between a third and two-thirds of this variation: older topics exhibit stronger preferential attachment, self-citation, and gender homophily, and topics with a higher share of male authors exhibit stronger self-citation. The results caution against treating network mechanisms estimated on pooled data as uniform, and C-RHEMs offer a scalable way to study how they differ across research areas.

Fully Bayesian Benchmarking for Small Area Models with Count Data

Soumadeep Bhowmick, Anindya Roy, Gauri S. Datta, Kyle M. Irimata, Jerry J. Maples

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Small area estimates of counts are often required to conform exactly to reliable aggregates at a higher geographic level. Often the area-level model is specified on a transformed (nonlinear) scale such as a log scale. Specifying the small area model on the log-scale is common practice for positive responses. A prominent example is the production of the Small Area Income and Poverty Estimates (SAIPE) by the US Census Bureau. When the model is specified in a transformed scale, the benchmarking constraints, which are linear in the area means, become nonlinear in terms of the model parameters. We develop a new class of priors for Bayesian small area models under strict external benchmarking constraints when the small area model is specified in a transformed scale, and the model parameters belong to a Riemannian manifold due to the nonlinear benchmarking constraints. The prior is fully supported on the constrained parameter set, the manifold defined by the benchmarking constraints, and thereby provides a proper constrained posterior that is also supported on the restricted set. This facilitates full Bayesian analysis of the problem without the requirement of post-hoc modification to the posterior to enforce the benchmarking constraints.

Shengjie Niu, Yeheng Ge, Jian Huang

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Training-data auditing asks whether data of interest were used to train a large language model (LLM), a question central to privacy auditing, copyright protection, and benchmark-contamination detection. These concerns are particularly relevant to the use of copyrighted books and scientific papers in pre-training, as well as synthetic text and medical question-answer records in fine-tuning. In practice, training membership signals may be concentrated in only a few passages of a long document, making it important to aggregate evidence within each document without diluting these localized signals. Beyond individual documents, auditing a target collection requires an overall assessment based on the document-level evidence. In this paper, we propose a unified statistical framework for document-level membership inference, target-collection global auditing, and suspicious-subset identification. Under the stated exchangeability conditions, we establish finite-sample validity of document-level p-values, type-I error control for global auditing, and false discovery rate control for subset identification. Case studies on BookTection, Pile-arXiv, Cosmopedia, and HealthCareMagic demonstrate the effectiveness and reliability of the proposed framework in auditing pre-training and fine-tuning data.

stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Elementwise Precision-weighted Compositional Data Analysis by Weighted Alternating Least Squares

Jan Graffelman

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Logratio principal component is a multivariate method commonly used for exploring compositional data sets. This method is mostly used in an unweighted manner, employing the centred logratio transformation of the compositions. The smaller elements of a composition usually have a higher relative error, and can be downweighted using part-wise weighting. In this article we focus on weighting compositional data and propose elementwise precision weighting of compositions. This is particularly convenient for compositions with parts that fluctuate from rare to frequent; e.g. parts acting as traces in some samples, but being abundant in others. We suggest to weight in the logarithmic or logratio scale, with the inverse of the standard deviation of the logratio, the latter being estimated for each part of each composition separately. A weighted least squares algorithm is used for dimension reduction and compositional biplot construction. Several applications of elementwise precision weighting are described, using compositional datasets from genetics, archaeology and microbiomics that illustrate the power and advantages of a fully weighted compositional analysis.

Causal Effect Estimation Under Uncertain Statistical Surrogacy

Yi Liu, Alexander W. Levis, Shu Yang, Larry Han

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Surrogate endpoints can improve the efficiency of causal effect estimation, but such gains may depend on structural assumptions. One such assumption is statistical surrogacy, which requires the primary outcome to be conditionally independent of treatment given the surrogate and baseline covariates. We study causal effect estimation with multiple candidate surrogates of uncertain validity under statistical surrogacy, considering settings where the primary outcome is either fully observed or subject to missingness. We characterize the asymptotic efficiency differences between surrogate-assisted and benchmark estimators, examine the bias induced by violations of statistical surrogacy, and identify conditions under which incorporating surrogate information leads to efficiency gains or losses. To address uncertainty in surrogate validity, we propose Surrogate-assisted Adaptive Causal effect EStimation (SACES), a procedure that anchors estimation to a robust benchmark while adaptively selecting among a finite set of surrogate-assisted estimators using an estimated mean squared error criterion. SACES aims to leverage efficiency gains when statistical surrogacy holds while preserving consistency when the assumption is violated. Simulations demonstrate that SACES achieves a favorable bias-variance trade-off across varying degrees of surrogacy violation. We illustrate the proposed methods using data from the ACTG 175 clinical trial to demonstrate how surrogate information can be adaptively incorporated into causal effect estimation.

stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Interpreting the Morbidity Gap

Alec Morton

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The Global Burden of Disease (GBD) study now reports the morbidity gap (MG), life expectancy minus healthy life expectancy, as an indicator in its own right. Writing MG as the expected cumulative rate of years lived with disability (YLDs) at the life-table age at death, we use stochastic dominance results to show what MG measures, how it responds to mortality and how it relates to the YLDs of the current population. We show the following. (a) A YLD profile gives an MG no larger than another's under every life table if and only if its cumulative YLD rate is nowhere higher. (b) With YLD rates fixed, lower mortality at every age widens MG, so that removing a risk factor that raises mortality reduces MG by less than its contribution to MG in GBD's risk factor attribution. (c) A family of results gives easily interpretable conditions on the age structure of the population for the crude YLD rate to be above or below the life-table YLD rate, which is MG divided by life expectancy, and these conditions change with population ageing. We illustrate the results for Singapore, where MG widened from 8.9 to 11.2 years between 1990 and 2023.

Optimal Transport Rank Regression for Robust Multivariate-Response Regression

Yizhe Ding, Yifan Jiang, Runze Li, Lingzhou Xue

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In this paper, we propose optimal transport rank regression for robust multivariate-response mean regression. The proposed method maps residual distributions to the uniform measure on the unit ball and constructs its loss from the resulting optimal transport ranks. This construction exploits cross-response dependence to improve efficiency while preserving population Fisher consistency and orthogonal invariance. Beyond these population properties, we establish statistical guarantees for the proposed method in both linear and nonparametric regression. For linear models, we establish asymptotic normality, characterise robustness, and develop orthogonally invariant asymptotic inference. We derive a distribution-uniform lower bound on asymptotic efficiency relative to least-squares and demonstrate efficiency gains over coordinatewise methods under several heavy-tailed and contaminated distributions. For nonparametric regression, we establish non-asymptotic \(L^2(P)\) convergence rates and robustness guarantees modulo additive constants using deep neural networks under possibly asymmetric heavy-tailed noise, attaining the minimax-optimal rate up to logarithmic factors. Numerical experiments demonstrate improvements in estimation accuracy and inferential efficiency over coordinatewise and radial robust methods, alongside stability under orthogonal transformations of the responses. An oral microbiome application further shows that the proposed method identifies more associations that are significant in all examined orthonormal response representations compared with each competing method.

stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-20 · Rule-ranked

Stochastic counterpart of Nesterov's method for sampling and provable non-acceleration of samplers with heavy-ball structure

A. Sharma

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Polyak's heavy-ball method and Nesterov's method are two momentum-based methods that have inspired many optimization techniques used in applied fields, including machine learning. Momentum also provides a connection with sampling, and interestingly, many kinetic Langevin samplers reduce to the heavy-ball recurrence when velocity is eliminated and noise is removed. This observation motivates a natural question: what is the corresponding sampler for Nesterov's method? We propose a sampler whose deterministic part is exactly Nesterov's recurrence, driven by temporally correlated Gaussian noise and requires one new gradient evaluation per step. Noise correlation provides $\mathcal{O}(h^2)$ bias for smooth observables. We establish geometric ergodicity for possibly non-convex potentials. For strongly convex potentials with Lipschitz continuous gradients, we obtain contraction rates and show that, up to an additive constant, the energy decays at a rate of order $\kappa^{-1/2}$, where $\kappa$ is the condition number. For Gaussian targets, we show that relaxation has square-root dependence on $\kappa$. We further prove that any sampler based on heavy-ball dynamics with additive noise cannot achieve accelerated convergence uniformly over the class of strongly convex functions. Finally, we compare the proposed sampler with OBABO, BAOAB, HMC, MALA, BPS, and NUTS in numerical experiments and demonstrate the better performance of proposed sampler.

Realized versus Population Reliability: Anytime-Valid Self-Consistency Certification under Correlated Reasoning

Terence D. Agbeyegbe, Toneukarin G. Agbeyegbe

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Self-consistency certification treats repeated sampling from a language model as sequential evidence accumulation and stops when an anytime-valid e-process crosses a threshold, guaranteeing finite-sample Type I error control by Ville's inequality. Prior constructions assume sampled rollouts are conditionally independent. We show this assumption fails in practice: reasoning streams are positively correlated through shared reasoning prefixes or across-prompt heterogeneity, changing not merely certificate efficiency but the hypothesis certified. Modeling dependence via a per-unit latent state (the random-effects / intraclass-correlation structure) induces exchangeable, equi-correlated increments with correlation $\rho$. Correlation splits the single null into a realized null about the drawn belief and a population null about average model behavior, which coincide only when $\rho=0$. We show: (i) the standard betting e-process remains anytime-valid for the realized null, not the population null; (ii) read as a population-null test, a suitable mixture has asymptotic crossing probability at least $\pi=P(\mu(Z)>0)$, potentially exceeding nominal $\epsilon$; (iii) the within-unit mean's effective sample size saturates at $1/\rho$, so extra within-unit rollouts cannot restore population validity; and (iv) a prompt-level e-process restores population validity, with optimal allocation at one rollout per prompt under a pure rollout budget. Simulations and Claude Haiku 4.5 rollouts confirm this regime dependence: on GSM8K ($\hat{\rho}=0.435$, $\hat{\theta}=+0.96$) the gap is dormant, whereas on AIME ($\hat{\rho}=0.480$, $\hat{\theta}=-0.15$) the single-unit rule crosses on 24% of prompts at nominal $\epsilon=0.05$, while the prompt-level procedure declines (0.00). This correctness result identifies which reliability claim an anytime-valid certificate actually supports under dependence.

LIC-Audit: How Comparable Are Reported Gains in Learned Image Compression?

Haisheng Fu, Jie Liang, Runyu Yang, Feng Ding, Xin Dong, Zixiao Jin, Jingning Han

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Learned image compression (LIC) papers often report BD-rate improvements of only a few percent. They are measured against VTM reference software whose version keeps changing: the claims we audit name 7 versions, 5 in 2025 papers alone. But how comparable are these numbers across papers, and what evidence should future papers give for such small gains? We introduce LIC-Audit, an analysis of 353 released rate-distortion curves from 36 repositories, and a controlled re-evaluation of eight public codec releases with 28,193 encode-decode records. We identify three problems. First, different VTM reference runs change BD-rates by up to 3.56 percentage points, and on CLIC Pro published numbers order 10 of 55 method pairs differently from a common computation; comparing codecs directly over a fixed quality range removes this dependence. Second, releases use different and mostly undocumented conventions for counting bits, computing PSNR, and padding images. Under consistent measurement, the GLIC method's 1.00% lead over the HPCM method on Tecnick becomes a 1.43% deficit, changing only the padding reverses the comparison of HPCM and CMIC, and most gaps narrow when MS-SSIM replaces PSNR. Third, average curves hide how much results vary from image to image, so whether a small gain is statistically significant depends on the assumed variation. An optimistic assumption calls 12.0% of comparisons between equally good codecs significant on test sets as small as Kodak, instead of 5%; a cautious one leaves 18 of 45 differences between recent codecs undecided, yet per-image results show 12 of them to be significant. Beyond auditing past results, LIC-Audit provides a versioned evaluation protocol, a public leaderboard website with a scoring tool, source-linked benchmark data, per-image measurements, and reporting guidelines, to help future LIC papers make comparable and verifiable claims of progress.

Abstract and links

Attention maps cheaply show where a vision-language model (VLM) looks, but attention sinks make them unreliable for localization. Contrasting the question with a generic prompt suppresses sinks, yet only some layers localize the queried object. Existing methods find these layers with box annotations or fixed rules. The two effects separate exactly, and the second, measured in bits on unlabeled questions, indicates which layers localize. WhereBits turns these where-to-look bits into a layer prior and combines it with per-head contrastive scores. In training-free zoom-in, its crops raise V* accuracy by 6.0 points under forced-choice evaluation.

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Simultaneous deconvolution model is investigated when the design points are random and follow bounded densities and errors are either $i.i.d.$ or long-memory dependent. Wasserstein Target Diagram for Model Evaluation

Tiantian Yang, Dongwei Chen

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The target diagram is often used in geosciences, which provides a visualization tool for model evaluation and shows the temporal or spatial matching of data. However, its construction requires paired data of equal sample sizes, limiting its application to model comparisons involving unpaired samples or probability distributions. In this study, we propose the Wasserstein target diagram based on the 2-Wasserstein distance from optimal transport. Unlike the original target diagram, the Wasserstein target diagram does not require paired data of equal sizes and evaluates models from the probability distribution perspective. We further establish that the two target diagrams coincide when the given paired data are comonotone, which is common in climate science, such as cumulative precipitation and solar radiation. Through two simulation studies and an application to wind speed distribution fitting, we demonstrate that the proposed diagram can detect the probability distribution discrepancies that the original target diagram may not distinguish. The Wasserstein target diagram therefore lifts the model evaluation to the probability level and provides a complementary approach to estimating the agreement in probability distributions.

Balancing Partition Forest for Causal Weighting

Felipe Angelim, Alessandro Leite

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Balancing weights are used in observational causal inference to construct a population in which the treatment is independent of observed pretreatment covariates. The ideal weight is the ratio between the product of the covariate and treatment marginals and their joint distribution, for binary and continuous treatments alike. We propose Balancing Partition Tree (BPT) and Balancing Partition Forest (BPF), which estimate this ratio on a learned rectangular partition of covariate--treatment space. Each split maximizes the gain in a Bregman divergence computed from joint and marginal counts. Two choices of divergence yield criteria that maximize the mutual information captured by the partition or reduce the squared error of the weights. We show that the population residual of either criterion bounds the bias of inverse-probability-weighted and doubly robust response estimators that use the population partition weights. Across 17 synthetic and semi-synthetic settings and three response estimators, BPF obtains the lowest mean RMSE rank among seven weight estimators, and a single tree fits in 0.14 seconds on 16,512 observations.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Federated Differential Privacy with Batch Contamination

Jason K. C. Ng, Thomas B. Berrett, Yi Yu

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study mean estimation under federated differential privacy (FDP) constraints and batch contamination, considering both Gaussian and heavy-tailed distributions. In the homogeneous setting, where sample sizes and privacy budgets are constant across servers, we show that the costs of privacy and contamination disentangle. Under a bounded $v$th moment condition, with $v \in (1, \infty)$, the cost of batch contamination is of order $(\varepsilon/n)^{2-2/v}\vee \varepsilon^2/n$, where $\varepsilon$ is the contamination level and $n$ is the per-batch sample size. This reveals a phase transition at $\varepsilon\asymp n^{1-v/2}$, for $v > 2$, which is the first of its kind in the literature. We develop a two-stage procedure that achieves the minimax rate up to poly-logarithmic factors. A key component is a private smoothed voting procedure tailored to the FDP setting that provides optimal sensitivity control. For heterogeneous batch sample sizes and privacy budgets, we introduce a batch partitioning framework that groups batches with comparable per-batch sample sizes and privacy budgets. Surprisingly, the minimax rate is determined, up to poly-logarithmic factors, by the most informative group of batches. Consequently, in some regimes, it is minimax optimal to use only an arbitrarily small proportion of the data set. Heterogeneity also introduces interactions between the costs of privacy and contamination, in contrast to their disentanglement in the homogeneous setting. For unknown contamination level, we propose an adaptive, near-minimax optimal Lepski-type extension for the heterogeneous Gaussian setting, designed specifically to prevent privacy leakage by ensuring no repeated use of data points.
