Spatial Extremes on Domains with Physical Barriers

Chien-Chung Wang, Benjamin A. Shaby

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We propose a model for spatial extremes what realistically accommodates nonconvex domains. Most existing models assume that tail dependence between locations depends only on their separation. Even if extended to be anisotropic or nonstationary, they still cannot accommodate features between points that may mediate the dependence. This assumption limits their applicability in settings where physical barriers like mountains, coastlines, or islands may attenuate or even block dependence in extreme events. In this work, we use the idea of a visibility graph to modify an existing spatial random scale model. This results in a tail dependence model that naturally accommodates nonconvex spatial domains, where the nonconvex features are interpreted as physical barriers. Furthermore, the model inherits key properties from the scale mixture like the ability to represent asymptotic independence at long ranges and either asymptotic dependence or asymptotic independence at short ranges. We apply our model to two applications in which physical barriers feature prominently in determining the spatial structure of extreme events. The first is precipitation in the South Island of New Zealand, where the Southern Alps shelter the eastern region from the effects of atmospheric rivers which arrive from the west. The second is significant wave heights in the North Atlantic, where the Faroe Islands tend to block the effects of extratropical cyclones traveling into the North Sea.

Instrument indeterminacy: separating uncertainty in POVM effects and its neutrosophic reading

Fernando A. Otero

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Neutrosophic annotation frameworks for quantum measurement attach an external triple $(t,i,f)$ to each Born probability, and explicitly leave open how indeterminacy could be defined operationally. We show that indeterminacy cannot be recovered from outcome probabilities, but can be computed from the pair (state, effect). For a binary POVM $\{A, I-A\}$ we define the instrument indeterminacy $\mathcal{I}_\rho(A) = 4 \mathrm{Tr}(\rho(A-A^2))$, which vanishes exactly when the state only sees the sharp part of the effect. The normalized Gini index of the outcome splits as $4TF = \mathcal{I}_\rho(A) + 4 \mathrm{Var}_\rho(A)$, a law of total variance that separates the instrument contribution from the state contribution; the identity is a special case of Massar's POVM uncertainty operator, and our contribution is its neutrosophic reading. Moving the system-apparatus cut to a Naimark dilation transfers exactly $\mathcal{I}_\rho(A)$ from indeterminacy to variance, which yields the missing operational definition. We extend the decomposition to $m$-outcome POVMs, relate the minimum indeterminacy of two incompatible unbiased qubit observables to their commutator through Busch's joint-measurability criterion, and show that $\mathcal{I}_\rho(A)$ equals twice the disagreement probability of a repeated L\"uders measurement. The resulting estimator needs neither the state nor the effect and, in simulation, requires 2.5 to 6 times fewer shots than detector tomography for the same error.

Confounding Heterogeneity in Multivariate Sensitivity Analysis for Observational Studies

Anthony Frazier, Siyu Heng, Wen Zhou

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In observational studies, unmeasured confounding may bias causal conclusions even after matching on measured confounders. Sensitivity analysis is therefore usually needed to assess the robustness of causal conclusions to unmeasured confounding. We consider multivariate sensitivity analysis that assesses the causal effect of a single treatment on multiple outcomes. We identify an inherent feature of such analyses, which we call ``confounding heterogeneity'': different outcomes may be affected by distinct unmeasured confounders, and a common unmeasured confounder may affect different outcomes to varying degrees. Building on matched observational studies, we formulate sensitivity analysis frameworks that accommodate confounding heterogeneity. We show that Type I error control for many existing sensitivity analysis methods is guaranteed under sensitivity analysis models and modes of inference that differ fundamentally from those used in the classic framework. We study asymptotic power using design sensitivity and Bahadur-Rosenbaum efficiency and examine finite-sample power through numerical studies. Our results show that the structure of unmeasured confounding affects sensitivity analysis, and that widely used single-parameter frameworks may mischaracterize the sensitivity of a causal conclusion to unmeasured confounding when confounding heterogeneity is considered. Simulations and a data application illustrate the practical importance of confounding heterogeneity in multivariate sensitivity analyses.

When Do LLMs Model the Right Target? Problem Formulation in High-Stakes Risk Prediction

Jennifer Zhang, Amanda Coston

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Generative AI is increasingly used to assist with data science tasks, yet fundamental questions remain about its ability to correctly formulate analytical problems. We investigate how well large language models (LLMs) can formulate prediction problems when developing models for high-stakes decision making. We focus on one particular challenge to validity: decision dependency, which arises when historical decisions affect observed outcomes. We evaluate this aspect of problem formulation in LLM-generated code and explanations across child-welfare, healthcare, and employment settings, using 11 models from OpenAI and Anthropic and seven prompt conditions. We assess whether the LLM-generated code implements a procedure appropriate for estimating the intended outcome under the study's assumptions and whether the explanation acknowledges decision dependency. We find that LLMs do not reliably address decision dependency without guidance in the prompt. When prompts specify the target, the assumption needed to estimate it, and the estimation procedure, target-aligned implementation reaches 99-100%. Among frontier models, a simple prompt description of how decisions affect outcomes appears sufficient to elicit target-aligned implementation. Some responses also acknowledge decision dependency without addressing it in their code. Our findings suggest that analysts should not rely on LLMs to correctly formulate prediction problems without explicit methodological guidance and careful review. We propose validity-centered guidelines for LLM-assisted predictive modeling, covering prompt formulation, validation of the target and estimation procedure, and identification of key assumptions. Establishing validity is a necessary early step in evaluating whether to deploy a predictive model in a high-stakes setting, but deployment requires weighing broader ethical, institutional, and practical considerations.

A Multisite Super Learner for Survival Prediction with Limited Target-Site Data

Henry Bayly, Yorghos Tripodis, Haolin Li, Prasad Patil

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Survival prediction models developed in multi-site studies may transport poorly to a new site because of between-site heterogeneity in covariate effects and baseline hazards. Pooled models may fail to capture site-specific risk patterns, while site-specific models can be unstable in small cohorts. We propose a Multi-Site Super Learner (MSSL), a domain-adaptive ensemble for survival prediction that combines diverse pooled and site-specific base learners. A meta-learner optimizes the ensemble weights using source data alone or, if available, a small target-site calibration sample. In simulations, when even a small target-site sample was available, MSSL substantially reduced prediction error relative to a pooled super learner under moderate to large between-site heterogeneity and generally matched or improved upon models fit to the target-site data alone. It did so by downweighting dissimilar or noisy models and upweighting models from structurally similar sites. Without target-site data, MSSL did not improve upon the pooled super learner. In an application to predicting dementia progression using the National Alzheimer's Coordinating Center (NACC) Uniform Data Set across 10 sites, MSSL matched or outperformed standard super learning using pooled data in 9 of 10 sites. MSSL offers a practical framework for adapting multi-site survival prediction models to a new site when a small calibration sample is available.

Anytime-valid goodness-of-fit test by $e$-spacings

Yunda Hao, Kin Wai Chan

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

To perform an anytime-valid goodness-of-fit test, we propose spacing-based \(e\)-processes that control the type-I error rate under arbitrary stopping rules. Based on the maximum spacing of order statistics, we construct spacing-based \(e\)-processes for nonparametrically testing distribution. The resulting procedures are simple and computationally efficient, and are power-consistent. We call them $e$-spacing tests. This approach is especially powerful in detecting localized discrepancy between the data-generating density and the null density. Under mild conditions, the logarithm of the proposed \(e\)-process is asymptotically equivalent to that of the oracle-optimal \(e\)-process. We further generalize the construction from maximal spacing to maximum spacing set in the form of the \(1/k\) fraction of the largest \(m\)th order spacings, as a measure of the lower-level set of density. It boosts power under more global discrepancy from the null. Furthermore, we propose a greedy predictable scheme, via argmax or softmax rules, to combine multiple $e$-spacing testing strategies. We prove that it optimally tunes the $e$-spacing test. The proposed approach is readily generalizable to multivariate testing via a coordinate-wise Rosenblatt-transform. Simulation studies demonstrate that the proposed methods perform favorably relative to the considered anytime-valid competitors as well as various types of multiple test-adjusted Kolmogorov--Smirnov tests.

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper develops an Economic Progress Index (EPI), a composite measure built from Earth Observation (EO) and open geospatial data with unsupervised Principal Component Analysis (PCA) weighting. The index captures the observable productive and enabling capacity of local economies and the intensity with which economic space is used. Sub-national monitoring in developing economies is held back by sparse data: district GDP estimates arrive with multi-year lags, rely on price deflators and top-down allocation, and do not exist at the subdistrict (taluka/tehsil) scale where place-based policy is targeted. Because it measures structural capacity rather than annual output, the index kept rising through the FY2020-21 COVID-19 contraction. Across Maharashtra's six revenue divisions, relative inequality fell by 44% even as the absolute gap between leading and lagging divisions widened by 32%; district-level $\beta$-convergence implies a half-life of about 14 years. The framework offers a scalable, deflator-free basis for structural monitoring in data-sparse economies.

NOTREKS: Marginal Independence in Score-based Structure Learning

Fabian Bleile, Sarah Lumpp, Mathias Drton

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Score-based structure learning estimates causal graphs by optimizing a data-dependent objective over structural equation models. We study how prior knowledge that selected pairs of variables are marginally independent can be incorporated into such causal discovery methods. Under faithfulness, marginally independent pairs cannot be connected by a trek, so the knowledge becomes a graphical no-trek constraint, which we call NOTREKS information. We introduce a power-series trek (PSTrek) penalty for gradient-based optimization and a trek constraint for order-based search, and we analyze why such information helps in finite samples and how it restricts the search space. Our numerical experiments illustrate to what extent NOTREKS information helps in causal discovery. Is It Worth Collecting Additional Covariates?

Wen Zhang, Yanyuan Ma, Liping Zhu

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In many studies, basic covariates are routinely recorded at negligible cost, whereas acquiring additional covariates requires expensive or time-consuming procedures. Thus, a natural question is whether it is worth to collecting these additional covariates. Using the reduction in prediction interval width as criterion, we evaluate and test the gain brought by the additional covariates and hence answer this question. We consider the gain both marginally and conditionally on the observable characteristics. The conditional formulation captures subpopulation heterogeneity and supports individualized data acquisition decisions. To avoid restrictive parametric assumptions, we adopt a model-free framework that accommodates flexible nonparametric or machine learning methods. We propose an efficient influence function based method, with proven theoretical properties to achieve our purpose under mild nuisance convergence rates. Interestingly, we find the natural conformal prediction based method cannot achieve the same purpose: It fails in the marginal case and imposes much more stringent or even infeasible requirement on the nuisance parameter estimation procedures. We demonstrate the superior finite-sample performance of the proposed procedures through simulations and real data applications.

PIVOT: Distribution-Preserving Multi-BitWatermarking with Minimax-Optimal Scoring

Wen Zhang, Kaiyi Pang, Minhao Bai, Shuyu Gan, Liping Zhu

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

As LLM-generated content becomes increasingly widespread, reliable attribution calls for multi-bit watermarks that embed recoverable messages without distorting the output distribution. We introduce PIVOT, a multi-bit watermark with model-free verification based on mirrored rank-interval sampling. At each generation step, PIVOT uses a keyed pseudorandom scalar and a secret permutation of the vocabulary. The message bit determines whether next-token probability intervals are arranged in forward or reverse secret-rank order. Sampling from these mirrored layouts preserves every token probability exactly while inducing an opposite-signed dependence between the scalar and the sampled token's secret rank. This dependence yields a pivotal rank statistic whose null distribution is invariant to the unknown next-token law, enabling model-free calibration and decoding. We formulate joint watermark detection and message recovery as a multiple-hypothesis testing problem, derive a moment-robust minimax-optimal detection score over a relaxed moment class, and establish finite-sample recovery guarantees. Extensive experiments on two representative large language models validate the effectiveness of PIVOT, demonstrating reliable watermark detection and message recovery while preserving the generator's output distribution.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Fill-Distance-Guided Active Learning for Causal Function Recovery under Network Interference

Peiyao Liu, Hongtao Zhu, Sizhe Zhang, Yang Su, Zhenyu Zhao, Nan Chen

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Causal inference seeks to learn how outcomes change under alternative interventions, but network interactions complicate this task. Under network interference, outcomes depend on both own treatment and neighbors' treatments, making causal effects functions of neighborhood exposure rather than single treatment contrasts. Existing methods primarily target prespecified causal contrasts or their estimation precision, leaving global response-function recovery largely unexplored. We study sequential experimentation under a limited experimental budget, with the goal of recovering the full exposure-dependent population-average response function. To this end, we propose FANCI (Fill-Distance-Guided Active Learning for Network-Interference Causal Inference). For function learning, treatment-specific Gaussian processes flexibly model nonlinear conditional response surfaces. For experimental design, we formulate global response-function recovery as a coverage problem and propose a fill-distance criterion that guides new experiments toward poorly covered regions. To account for network constraints on attainable exposure patterns, we develop full-network and target-screened strategies for selecting feasible network--assignment pairs. Theoretically, we derive uniform error bounds linking fill distance to response-function estimation and characterize the approximation loss from target screening. Simulations and an online-game experiment show that FANCI improves recovery of nonlinear exposure-dependent effects and reveals treatment patterns obscured when network interference is ignored.

stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Fast Bayesian Methods for Uncertainty Quantification in Sparse AI Evaluation

Dario Deniz Ergun, Haohua Wang, Brendon Tao, Jonah Kollenberg, Chen Ye Gan

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Estimating how an AI system will perform on a new benchmark need not require another large model. We use public evaluation counts to choose a weak Beta prior, then update it exactly from at most 31 revealed outcomes. The predictor uses no item semantics, pretrained weights, or inference API. Its single online evaluation in the AIMS competition returned a team-reported Brier ALC of 0.188477, a 24.6% reduction from the constant-0.5 reference. The complete submission is 7,754 bytes; a server microbenchmark measures 12.71 microseconds per prediction with 31 matching labels. We describe how source evaluations are compressed into 301 prior means and show why a concentration of two lets new evidence rapidly determine the prediction. The resulting posterior gives exact uncertainty about the modelled success rate without numerical integration or neural inference. A public evaluation covers 79 system-benchmark pairs and eight seeds, reaching Brier ALC 0.200250 for the submitted method. Accompanying code and sufficient statistics reconstruct the fitted prior byte for byte and replay all 632 evaluation trajectories. The result demonstrates how much predictive value a compact Bayesian model can extract from sparse target feedback at very low online cost.

stat.AP · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

A probabilistic analysis of the Brumadinho tailings dam-breach using uncertainty quantification and global sensitivity analysis

Yuri Taglieri S\'ao, Geraldo de Freitas Maciel, Julian Cardoso Eleut\'erio

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

On 25th January 2019, Dam B1 in Brumadinho, Brazil, suddenly collapsed and produced a catastrophic tailings flow with severe human, environmental and infrastructural consequences. Previous numerical back-analyses of the tailings flow have relied on deterministic approaches, which provide useful reference results but do not explicitly incorporate uncertainty associated to rupture hydrograph and tailings' physical properties. This work presents a probabilistic approach to study the tailings dam-breach flow caused by the rupture of Dam B1, focusing on the quantification and interpretation of the effects of input uncertainty on hazard-related hydrodynamical outputs. Deterministic data was obtained through HEC-RAS v6.6 by considering tailings as an one-phase Bingham fluid. The probabilistic framework combines dimensionality reduction, metamodeling and flooding classification to perform fast prediction of unexplored input scenarios. It allows to obtain data distributions that reflect output uncertainty and to estimate variance-based sensitivity indices to quantify the influence of input uncertainty over output variability. Results show that uncertainty is strongly dependent on time and spatial location. Hydrograph-related parameters mainly control the timing and magnitude of the early flow, whereas yield stress becomes more influential in regions associated with flow attenuation, spreading and possible arrest. Overall, the framework provides probabilistic hazard estimates and insights into the physical mechanisms governing the dynamics of tailings flow.

DOA Estimation in Antenna Arrays: Super-Resolution Methods in a Common Space

Sergey S. Kuzin

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper introduces a unified framework for angular super-resolution in antenna arrays. By integrating linear prediction beyond the physical aperture, entropy variations, and harmonic spatial spectra averaging, we recast classical methods - including Capon, Pisarenko, Maximum Entropy, and MUSIC - into a single structure. We extend this framework to accommodate ESPRIT-type methods by treating the number of simultaneously predicted subarray channels as an additional dimension. Furthermore, we adapt this approach to non-Gaussian signals, establishing a parallel framework for Virtual-ESPRIT-type methods. As a practical application, we propose universal, J-orthogonalization-based algorithms that estimate spatial super- resolution spectra within a unified computational architecture. This architecture produces spatial spectrum estimates for multiple super-resolution methods and any intermediate variations.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Latent space dynamic network actor models with position-rate link

Alberto Caimo, Isabella Gollini

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Relational event data record time-stamped interactions between actors. Dynamic network actor models represent each interaction as two separate decisions: when an actor becomes active and whom they select. Modelling these decisions separately cannot establish whether the most active actors are also the most frequently selected. We propose a latent space dynamic network actor model in which sender and receiver positions capture receiver choice, while a position-rate link relates latent positions to activity levels. The link uses two summaries invariant to latent position updates: latent popularity, measuring how much an actor is favoured by others, and excess self-affinity, capturing preference for similar actors beyond that explained by popularity. We adopt a Bayesian modular approach, estimating latent positions from receiver choices alone. Using e-mail data from a manufacturing company, we find that the most active employees are also the most frequently selected recipients. With multiple latent dimensions, preference for similar colleagues contributes less. Latent positions improve receiver-choice prediction, while the position-rate link captures differences in actor activity.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

Design-Based Inference for Fuzzy Difference-in-Differences

Pedro Picchetti

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

I study fuzzy difference-in-differences designs from a design-based perspective, in which potential outcomes and treatment decisions are fixed and only the assignment of a policy is random, with heterogeneous and possibly unknown assignment probabilities. The Wald-DiD design estimand equals an assignment-weighted complier effect plus contamination from switchers, from changes in always-takers' effects and from trends in untreated outcomes. I derive the conditions under which the estimand holds a clear causal interpretation. The conditions are weaker than parallel trends in both outcomes and treatments. We propose an Anderson--Rubin-type test with a conservative Neyman variance that remains valid under weak first stages, a sensitivity analysis for the complier effect, and estimators for designs with known assignment probabilities. An application to the Oregon Health Insurance Experiment illustrates the results.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

FRCI: An R Package for fuzzy and randomized confidence intervals

Carlos Henrique Trigo Nasser Felix, Nancy Lopes Garcia, Alex Rodrigo dos Santos Sousa

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Fuzzy confidence intervals provide a flexible framework for statistical inference by representing uncertainty through membership functions rather than fixed interval endpoints. Closely related to randomized confidence intervals, they offer an alternative approach for dealing with the conservativeness that frequently arises in discrete statistical models. Despite their attractive theoretical properties, the practical use of fuzzy and randomized confidence intervals has been limited by the lack of readily available software implementations. This paper introduces FRCI (Fuzzy and Randomized Confidence Intervals), an R package available on CRAN for constructing and visualizing fuzzy and randomized confidence intervals. The package implements confidence interval procedures for parameters of the normal, Bernoulli/binomial, and Poisson distributions, including a recently proposed method based on reference value choice, Geyer-Meeden, Agresti-Coull, and score methods. The main function computes the membership function associated with a selected confidence interval procedure, while dedicated graphical tools facilitate visualization and comparison of fuzzy confidence intervals. We describe the statistical background underlying the implemented methods, discuss the package design, and illustrate its use through several examples. The FRCI package provides researchers, practitioners, and students with an accessible and unified environment for the study and application of fuzzy and randomized confidence intervals.

Advancing mixed-effects random forests for predictions based on multi-cohort data

Jiumeng Zhang, Jordan Behrendt, Tanja Schultz, Tanja Vrijkotte, Siyu Zhou, Iris Pigeot, Claudia Bornhorst

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Pooling longitudinal cohorts can extend age coverage and improve long-term health predictions, but cohort heterogeneity complicate estimation and prediction. Mixed-effects machine learning (ME-ML) combines flexible machine learning methods with mixed-effect models to account for correlated observations, yet these ME-ML methods are limited in their applicability in complex settings such as multi-cohort data. We propose a multi-cohort mixed-effects random forest (MERFmulti-cohort) for improving predictions of individual health trajectories beyond the observed period. The method extends standard MERF through (i) better separation of population-level and subject-specific variation, (ii) flexibility in correcting systematic discrepancies in fixed-effects estimates to improve covariance parameter estimation, and (iii) flexible specification of random-effects and residual variance-covariance structures. We evaluate MERFmulti-cohort against linear regression, linear mixed models, random forests, and standard MERF in simulations and four real-data scenarios predicting BMI z-scores in children and adolescents. In simulations, MERFmulti-cohort improves covariance parameter estimation across several covariance structures while achieving prediction accuracy comparable to standard MERF. Predictive gains are most obvious when residual variances vary across study waves. In real-data applications, MERFmulti-cohort improves predictive accuracy relative to MERF by flexibly capturing complex patterns and accommodating cohort heterogeneity. Pooling similar cohorts covering extended age coverage can improve future individual-level predictions if the heterogeneity in multi-cohort data is adequately accounted for.

A Local False Discovery Rate Framework for Compound p-values

Jierui Zhu, Rina Foygel Barber, Nikolaos Ignatiadis

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Compound p-values relax individual p-value validity to an average condition across the null hypotheses. This flexibility, however, need not protect particular rejections, especially when paired with an average error criterion such as the false discovery rate (FDR). In this work, we introduce "density-compound p-values," a concept that connects compound p-values and the frequentist local FDR paradigm. Under independence, we establish boundary FDR (bFDR) guarantees for the support-line (SL) procedure, with nominal-level control for weighted and compound-e-weighted p-values. For general density-compound p-values, our bounds depend on a tractable null-distribution parameter, and we show that without further restrictions on this parameter, the bFDR can substantially exceed its nominal level. Our theory extends to discrete compound p-values constructed by pooling permutation tests. Applications illustrate the method in low-resolution testing problems, where individually valid permutation p-values are too coarse to yield discoveries after multiplicity correction.

The Structural Number of Parties: An Electoral-Outcome-Independent Baseline for Comparing Party-System Scale

Adama Nouboukpo, Kodzo Michel Aladji, Bappa Muktar

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

How many parties can a country structurally sustain? Standard measures such as the effective number of parties (ENP) cannot answer this question. They are descriptive and post hoc: they summarize competition only after an election, and they depend on electoral returns that are often missing, interrupted, or unreliable in many developing democracies, particularly in Africa. They also offer no common scale for comparing the size of party registers with realized electoral fragmentation. We introduce the Structural Number of Parties (SNP), an outcome-independent baseline derived from two universally available quantities: population size and territorial area. Building on the logic of the seat product model and the cube-root law of assembly size, the SNP gives an ex ante benchmark that can be computed for any country and year, including where electoral data are absent. Applied to African states, the SNP enables a structural gap diagnosis that distinguishes under-fragmented systems, marked by hegemonic-party dominance, from over-fragmented ones, marked by excessive atomization. The framework thus provides a transparent and reproducible tool to inform institutional reform and the rationalization of party systems across the continent.

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The spatial distribution of infrastructure and activities, along with the networks and interactions that connect them, is key to understanding the relationship between urban form, function, and sustainability from a complex systems perspective. Within this dynamic relationship, the extent to which localized social infrastructure can reinforce existing functional centralities or create conditions for the emergence of new ones remains insufficiently understood. This study examines these relationships through the UTOP\'IAS program in Iztapalapa, Mexico City, where 15 public social facilities have been established across a historically underserved urban territory. We develop a multilayer network framework integrating pedestrian infrastructure, GTFS-based public transport services, and UTOP\'IAS as a target layer to assess multimodal connectivity, travel-time accessibility, and spatial coverage. stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-19 · Rule-ranked

On the Validity of Fisher Randomization Tests under the Weak Null

Timothy Sudijono, Edgar Dobriban, Eric Tchetgen Tchetgen

Relevance 0/10. Matched topics: none; keyword matches: 0; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Consider the problem of testing the Neyman weak null of no sample average treatment effect $H_{0N}:\tau = 0$ in randomized experiments with binary outcomes. Although the Fisher Randomization Test (FRT) is typically justified under Fisher's sharp null of no individual treatment effect, we show that it is finite-sample-valid for the weak null $H_{0N}$ in three common combinations of study design and test statistic: 1) balanced completely randomized experiments with the difference-in-means test statistic, 2) matched pairs experiments with the same test statistic, and 3) balanced Bernoulli randomized experiments with the centered Horvitz-Thompson test statistic. As an immediate corollary, Fisher's exact test in a balanced completely randomized experiment is valid under the weak null, and so is McNemar's test in a matched pairs experiment. Similar results hold for the one-sided null $H_0:\tau \leq 0$. Beyond balanced designs, we show that twice the one-sided FRT $p$-value with the same test statistics is valid under $H_{0N}$. In simulation experiments, the FRTs compare favorably to other exact procedures.

Ana Carolina da Cruz, Camila P. E. de Souza, Pedro H. T. O. Sousa

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionFunctional data and local sparsity

Relevance 8/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Functional data and local sparsity; keyword matches: 4; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In practical regression applications, multiple covariates are often measured, but not all may be associated with the response variable. Identifying and including only the relevant covariates in the model is crucial for improving prediction accuracy. In this work, we develop a variational inference approach for estimation and variable selection in scalar-on-function regression, involving only functional covariates, and in partially functional regression models that also include scalar covariates. Specifically, we develop a variational expectation-maximization (VEM) algorithm, with a variational Bayes procedure implemented in the E-step to obtain approximate marginal posterior distributions for most model parameters, except for the regularization parameters, which are updated in the M-step. We further illustrate its practical utility through real-data applications involving spectral analysis of sugar samples and weather measurements from Japan.

Sparse penalized quantile regression provides an effective framework for variable selection and robust estimation in high-dimensional data analysis. Abstract and links

Expectile regression is a powerful tool for capturing heterogeneous structures in data and has attracted increasing attention in large-scale data analysis due to its computational efficiency. In this paper, we propose a debiased online expectile regression method for high-dimensional streaming data. Fuming Lin WEilin Mou

Abstract and links

In this paper, we consider high-dimensional Lp-quantile regression which only requires a low order moment of the error and is also a natural generalization of the above methods and Lp-regression as well. The loss function of Lp-quantile regression circumvents the non-differentiability of the absolute loss function and the difficulty of the squares loss function requiring the finiteness of error's variance and thus promises excellent properties of Lp-quantile regression. Specifically, we first develop a new method called composite Lp-quantile regression(CLpQR). We study the oracle model selection theory based on CLpQR (call the estimator CLpQR-oracle) and show in some cases of p CLpQR-oracle behaves better than CQR-oracle (based on composite quantile regression) when error's variance is infinite. Moreover, CLpQR has high efficiency and can be sometimes arbitrarily more efficient than both CQR and the least squares regression. Second, we propose another new regression method,i.e. near quantile regression and prove the asymptotic normality of the estimator when p converges to 1 and the sample size infinity simultaneously. As its applications, a new thought of smoothing quantile objective functions and a new estimation are provided for the asymptotic covariance matrix of quantile regression. Third, we develop a unified efficient algorithm for fitting high-dimensional Lp-quantile regression by combining the cyclic coordinate descent and an augmented proximal gradient algorithm. Remarkably, the algorithm turns out to be a favourable alternative of the commonly used liner programming and interior point algorithm when fitting quantile regression.

Estimation of multiple precision matrices under shared support with heterogeneous edge strengths

Sayan Ranjan Bhowal, Debashis Paul, Gopal K Basak, Samarjit Das

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 3; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Estimating multiple precision matrices in high-dimension presents significant challenges, particularly when distinct datasets share a common conditional dependency structure but exhibit population-specific interaction strengths. We address this problem by introducing the Multiplicative Graphical Lasso (Mglasso), a method for jointly estimating precision matrices across multiple Gaussian graphical models under a shared sparsity constraint. Each precision matrix is decomposed as a Schur-Hadamard product of a shared structural matrix $\boldsymbolΘ$, which encodes the common conditional independence graph, and a population-specific matrix $\boldsymbolΓ_{l}$, which captures variation in edge strengths across populations. We optimize a penalized log-likelihood that utilizes an $\ell_1$-penalty to enforce common sparsity and a Frobenius norm penalty to regulate population-specific variations. The optimization is efficiently performed using the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) algorithm integrated with gradient descent. Theoretically, we establish the local strict convexity of the objective function and provide rigorous high-dimensional consistency guarantees, including supremum norm error bounds and exact support recovery under sub-Gaussian tail conditions. Extensive simulations show superior model selection consistency at smaller sample sizes compared to the benchmark Group Graphical Lasso (GGL). Finally, the method's practical utility is further validated through real-world applications.

Stochastic Variational Inference for Vine Copula Distributional Regression

Gianmarco Callegher, Thomas Kneib

Quantile and distributional regressionVariational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 7.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 3; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Structured additive distributional regression flexibly relates all parameters of a conditional response distribution to covariates, but multivariate extensions remain challenging when dependence is complex. We propose a multivariate structured additive distributional regression model based on regular vine copulas. Different vine edges may use different pair-copula families, while every pair-copula parameter may vary with covariates through a structured additive predictor. The model therefore accommodates heterogeneous marginal distributions together with pair-specific asymmetric, tail-dependent, and covariate-dependent dependence structures. For scalable inference, we develop a tree-wise stochastic variational inference procedure based on component-specific Gaussian variational approximations. Marginal models are estimated first, followed by pair-copula regressions sequentially along the vine trees. We also adapt sequential vine selection by fitting candidate covariate-dependent pair-copula regressions and using information criteria based on effective degrees of freedom for both family and tree selection. In simulations, the tree-wise estimator remains close to an oracle using the true recursive conditional inputs, whereas global refinement yields more concentrated approximations and lower frequentist coverage for upstream components. An application to six-dimensional meteorological data from the Netherlands yields a vine combining Gaussian and non-Gaussian pair copulas, with pronounced nonlinear spatial and temporal variation in dependence.

stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-05 · Rule-ranked

A Unified Coordinate Descent Framework for Penalized Longitudinal Log-Contrast Quantile Regression

Changwei Du, Linhui Li, Huijuan Ma, Qi Zheng

Quantile and distributional regressionSparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 3; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Quantile regression characterizes heterogeneous covariate effects across the response distribution, but its extension to high-dimensional longitudinal compositional data poses substantial challenges, such as the nonsmooth check loss, the unit-sum structure of compositional covariates. Existing work is confined to Lasso penalization, and does not readily extend to nonconvex penalties. We propose CDaLCqr, a unified coordinate descent algorithm for penalized longitudinal log-contrast quantile regression accommodating popular penalties, such as adaptive Lasso, smoothly clipped absolute deviation (SCAD), and minimax concave penalty (MCP) within a single framework. The proposed method uses a Huber-type smooth approximation of the check loss and enforces the zero-sum constraint through an augmented Lagrangian formulation. CDaLCqr combines coordinate descent with majorization-minimization, yielding explicit updates through coordinate-wise quadratic surrogates. We show that the stopping tolerance controls errors in the zero-sum constraint and stationarity conditions. Under suitable conditions and sufficiently close initialization, we also prove local convergence of the coefficient and multiplier iterates, with an error bound decreasing by a fixed factor less than one each iteration. Simulations highlight the estimation advantages of SCAD and MCP variants and their sparse model identification. An application to Chinese provincial industrial data identifies interpretable, quantile-specific associations between sectoral composition and regional economic output.

Efficient Covariance Estimation for Sparsified Functional Data

Sijie Zheng, Fandong Meng, Jie Zhou

Abstract and links

Motivated by recent work involving the analysis of leveraging spatial correlations in sparsified mean estimation, we present a novel procedure for constructing covariance estimator. The proposed Random-knots (Random-knots-Spatial) and B-spline (Bspline-Spatial) estimators of the covariance function are computationally efficient. Asymptotic pointwise of the covariance are obtained for sparsified individual trajectories under some regularity conditions. Our proposed nonparametric method well perform the functional principal components analysis for the case of sparsified data, where the number of repeated measurements available per subject is small. In contrast, classical functional data analysis requires a large number of regularly spaced measurements per subject. Model selection techniques, such as the Akaike information criterion, are used to choose the model dimension corresponding to the number of eigenfunctions in the model. Theoretical results are illustrated with Monte Carlo simulation experiments. Finally, we cluster multi-domain data by replacing the covariance function with our proposed covariance estimator during PCA.

Learning Conditional Independence Differential Graphs From Time-Dependent Data

Jitendra K Tugnait

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimationDynamic and spatial modelsOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation, Dynamic and spatial models, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Estimation of differences in conditional independence graphs (CIGs) of two time series Gaussian graphical models (TSGGMs) is investigated where the two TSGGMs are known to have similar structure. The TSGGM structure is encoded in the inverse power spectral density (IPSD) of the time series. In several existing works, one is interested in estimating the difference in two precision matrices to characterize underlying changes in conditional dependencies of two sets of data consisting of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations. In this paper we consider estimation of the difference in two IPSDs to characterize the underlying changes in conditional dependencies of two sets of time-dependent data. Our approach accounts for data time dependencies unlike past work. We analyze a penalized D-trace loss function approach in the frequency domain for differential graph learning, using Wirtinger calculus. We consider both convex (group lasso) and non-convex (log-sum and SCAD group penalties) penalty/regularization functions. An alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) algorithm is presented to optimize the objective function. We establish sufficient conditions in a high-dimensional setting for consistency (convergence of the inverse power spectral density to true value in the Frobenius norm) and graph recovery. Both synthetic and real data examples are presented in support of the proposed approaches. In synthetic data examples, our log-sum-penalized differential time-series graph estimator significantly outperformed our lasso based differential time-series graph estimator which, in turn, significantly outperformed an existing lasso-penalized i.i.d. modeling approach, with $F_1$ score as the performance metric.

Antoine Barbieri, Angelo Alcaraz, Mouna Abed, Hugues de Courson, Hélène Jacqmin-Gadda

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection, Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The systematic collection of longitudinal data is very common in practice, making mixed models widely used. Most developments around these models focus on modeling the mean trajectory of repeated measurements, typically under the assumption of homoskedasticity. However, as data become increasingly rich through intensive collection over time, these models can become limiting and may introduce biases in analysis. In fact, such data are often heterogeneous, with the presence of outliers, heteroskedasticity, and asymmetry in the distribution of individual measurements. Therefore, ignoring these characteristics can lead to biased modeling results. In this work, we propose a mixed-effect distributional regression model based on the asymmetric Laplace distribution to: (1) address the presence of outliers, heteroskedasticity, and asymmetry in longitudinal measurements; (2) model the entire individual distribution of the heterogeneous longitudinal response over time, rather than just its conditional expectation; and (3) give a more comprehensive evaluation of the impact of covariates on the distribution of the responses through meaningful indicator. A Bayesian estimation procedure is presented. In order to choose between two distributional regression models, we also propose a new model selection criterion for longitudinal data. It measures the proximity between the individual distribution estimated by the model and the empirical individual distribution of the data over time, using a set of quantiles. The estimation procedure and the selection criterion are validated in a simulation study and the proposed model is compared to a distributional regression mixed model based on the Gaussian distribution and a location-scale linear quantile mixed model. Finally, the proposed model is applied to analyze blood pressure over time for hospitalized patients in the intensive care unit.

Variational approximate penalized credible regions for Bayesian grouped regression

Weichang Yu, Khue-Dung Dang

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We develop a fast and accurate grouped penalized credible region approach for variable selection and prediction in Bayesian high-dimensional regression. Most existing Bayesian methods either are subject to high computational costs due to long Markov Chain Monte Carlo runs or yield ambiguous variable selection results due to non-sparse solution output. The penalized credible region framework yields sparse post-processed estimates that facilitates unambiguous grouped variable selection. High estimation accuracy is achieved by shrinking noise from unimportant groups using a grouped global-local shrinkage prior. To ensure computational scalability, we approximate posterior summaries using coordinate ascent variational inference and recast the penalized credible region framework as a convex optimization problem that admits efficient computations. We prove that the resultant post-processed estimators are both parameter-consistent and variable selection consistent in high-dimensional settings. Theory is developed to justify running the coordinate ascent algorithm for at least two cycles. Through extensive simulations, we demonstrate that our proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art methods in grouped variable selection, prediction, and computation time for several common models including ANOVA and nonparametric varying coefficient models.

Leveraging Sparsity to Improve No-U-Turn Sampling Efficiency for Hierarchical Bayesian Models

Cole C. Monnahan, Kasper Kristensen, James T. Thorson, Bob Carpenter

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Analysts routinely use Bayesian hierarchical models to understand natural processes. The no-U-turn sampler (NUTS) is the most widely used algorithm to sample high-dimensional, continuously differentiable models. But NUTS is slowed by high correlations, especially in high dimensions, limiting the complexity of applied analyses. Here we introduce Sparse NUTS (SNUTS), which preconditions (decorrelates and descales) posteriors using a sparse precision matrix ($Q$). We use Template Model Builder (TMB) to efficiently compute $Q$ from the mode of the Laplace approximation to the marginal posterior, then pass the preconditioned posterior to NUTS through the Bayesian software Stan for sampling. We apply SNUTS to seventeen diverse case studies to demonstrate that preconditioning with $Q$ converges one to two orders of magnitude faster than Stan's industry standard diagonal or dense preconditioners. SNUTS also outperforms preconditioning with the inverse of the covariance estimated with Pathfinder variational inference. SNUTS does not improve sampling efficiency for models with the highly varying curvature found in funnels, wide tails, or multiple modes. SNUTS is most advantageous, and can be scaled beyond $10^4$ parameters, in the presence of high dimensionality, sparseness, and high correlations, all of which are widespread in applied statistics. An open-source implementation of SNUTS is provided in the R package SparseNUTS.

Rongke Lyu, Marina Vannucci, Suprateek Kundu

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

As a general and robust alternative to traditional mean regression models, quantile regression avoids the assumption of normally distributed errors, making it a versatile choice when modeling outcomes such as cognitive scores that typically have skewed distributions. Motivated by an application to Alzheimer's disease data where the aim is to explore how brain-behavior associations change over time, we propose a novel Bayesian tensor quantile regression for high-dimensional longitudinal imaging data. The proposed approach distinguishes between effects that are consistent across visits and patterns unique to each visit, contributing to the overall longitudinal trajectory. A low-rank decomposition is employed on the tensor coefficients which reduces dimensionality and preserves spatial configurations of the imaging voxels. We incorporate multiway shrinkage priors to model the visit-invariant tensor coefficients and variable selection priors on the tensor margins of the visit-specific effects. For posterior inference, we develop a computationally efficient Markov chain Monte Carlo sampling algorithm. Simulation studies reveal significant improvements in parameter estimation, feature selection, and prediction performance when compared with existing approaches. In the analysis of the Alzheimer's disease data, the flexibility of our modeling approach brings new insights as it provides a fuller picture of the relationship between the imaging voxels and the quantile distributions of the cognitive scores.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-04-16 · Rule-ranked

Sample continuation in Bayesian hierarchical model via variational inference

Yucong Liu, Zilai Si, Alexander Strang

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionHierarchical and mixed modelsOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Hierarchical and mixed models, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Posterior distributions arising in ill-posed Bayesian inverse problems are often both analytically intractable and highly sensitive to parameters of the chosen prior family. We aim to understand the sensitivity of intractable posterior distributions to changes in prior assumptions by tracking how a sample representation of the posterior changes as the prior parameters change. This enables sensitivity analysis for small perturbations in the prior, providing insights into the robustness of the posterior estimates under minor changes in assumptions. It also allows solution continuation when dealing with significant alterations in prior beliefs, facilitating a comprehensive understanding of how large shifts in assumptions affect the posterior distribution. We focus on a class of non-conjugate hierarchical models tailored to encourage sparsity in linear inverse problems. The specific hierarchical model of interest is chosen since it is parameterized by a small number of shape parameters, and includes most classical sparsity promoting priors as special cases. As the shape parameters change, the posterior can transition continuously from a tractable unimodal distribution to an intractable multimodal distribution. To track the change in the posterior, we adopt particle based variational inference methods, specifically Stein Variational Gradient Descent (SVGD). SVGD iteratively updates a set of samples to minimize the KL-divergence away from a desired target distribution. We augment SVGD by Birth-Death sampling, which can efficiently exchange mass between separated modes, while simultaneously optimizing the kernel bandwidth used to derive the SVGD update. This method enables the discovery of new modes by tracing the modes as they branch out of a simpler, unimodal posterior, derived within the same family of priors.

Change-point detection in variance-covariance matrix

Ying Lin, Benjamin Poignard

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We consider the joint estimation of change-point locations and the sparsity pattern of the variance-covariance matrix, which is assumed to evolve in a piecewise constant manner. By applying Group Fused LASSO and LASSO penalties to the squared Frobenius norm, we estimate both the covariance structure and the change points. Adaptive weights are incorporated into the penalty terms to enhance change-point detection and covariance estimation accuracy. We establish the conditions under which the estimated change points and the sparse estimators within each segment are consistent. To solve the resulting optimization problem efficiently, we develop an alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) whose updates reduce to computationally tractable subproblems. The performance of the proposed method is illustrated through synthetic and real-data experiments, including comparisons with several competing procedures.

Abstract and links

The Small BAseline Subset technique provides remote measurements of ground displacement with high spatial resolution, making it a key tool for monitoring geophysical processes in hazard-prone areas. An effective analysis of this type of data requires reliable estimation of their second-order structure, which is difficult to achieve because the measurements are systematically missing over relatively large portions of the investigated areas. We tackle the problem from a functional data analysis perspective and treat the observations as partially observed functional data with two-dimensional domain. To properly characterize the data, we introduce the fragmented regime of partial observation, where parts of the curves are systematically missing across replicates. Deep and Probabilistic Models for Gene Regulatory Network Inference

Claudia Skok Gibbs

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Gene regulatory networks (GRNs) link transcription factor (TF) proteins to their target genes, yet reconstructing these networks from genome-wide data remains challenging under practical and methodological constraints. Many methods couple modeling assumptions to a specific inference procedure and rely on heuristic model selection, while evaluation is constrained by incomplete reference networks and point-estimate outputs that lack uncertainty. GRN reconstruction also depends on prior knowledge to constrain TF-gene interactions, yet available priors are often assay-dependent and difficult to transfer across species and less-characterized systems. In this thesis, we develop two complementary frameworks that address these limitations. In the first, PMF-GRN casts GRN inference as a probabilistic graphical model optimized by variational inference, enabling principled model selection and uncertainty-aware edge estimates. In the second, GLM-Prior addresses the prior bottleneck by fine-tuning the pretrained Nucleotide Transformer to predict TF-target gene interactions directly from nucleotide sequence, while generalizing across yeast, mouse, and human settings. Together, PMF-GRN and GLM-Prior motivate a dual-stage view of GRN reconstruction in which sequence-derived priors provide a transferable starting scaffold and probabilistic inference refines regulatory estimates with quantified uncertainty under incomplete evaluation resources.

We present the R package exdqlm for Bayesian quantile regression, with primary emphasis on dynamic state-space quantile models for time series. Exact Generalization Error Curves of Kernel Ridge Regression for Functional Moment Estimation

Yinan Ding, Yicheng Li

Sparsity and model selectionFunctional data and local sparsityNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Functional data and local sparsity, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Kernel ridge regression is a standard method for functional data analysis, but its exact behavior is less understood. We study tensor-product kernel ridge regression for estimating the $r$-th moment function of a random function based on noisy discrete observations. The formulation includes mean estimation, covariance estimation, and higher-order moment estimation in a single framework. Our main result gives a precise $1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ expansion for the $L^2$ error at each admissible regularization parameter. The expansion consists of bias and three variance terms corresponding respectively to variation across the independent sample paths, latent signal variation at each sample point, and variation from measurement errors, identifying the refined error structure underlying functional data. As applications, we show that KRR attains the minimax rate for source smoothness $s \leq 2$ but becomes suboptimal in the sparse regime for $s>2$ due to saturation. A technical ingredient is a set of concentration inequalities for $U$-statistics suited to the dependent product structure of functional observations.

Distributed Stochastic Smoothing ADMM for Penalized Quantile Regression

Rongmei Liang, Xiaofei Wu

Quantile and distributional regressionSparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Quantile regression is well suited to heterogeneous and heavy-tailed data, but computation becomes challenging for large, distributed data sets because the check loss is nonsmooth. We propose a distributed stochastic smoothing alternating direction method of multipliers (DSS-ADMM) for horizontally partitioned penalized quantile regression. Each worker computes a mini-batch gradient of a Huber-smoothed check loss, and a coordinator performs a single proximal aggregation step for the regularizer. Raw observations remain local, worker updates run in parallel, and the method requires no matrix inversion. For proper, closed, and convex penalties, stacking the local coefficient vectors yields a standard two-block stochastic ADMM formulation. With fixed smoothing, we establish an expected $O(\log K/\sqrt K)$ joint objective-feasibility bound and an explicit $\eps/4$ approximation term for the original check-loss objective; when the smooth block is strongly convex, the bound improves to $O(\log K/K)$. We also characterize the scope of an extension to the minimax concave penalty and the smoothly clipped absolute deviation penalty. Reproducible simulations consider both homogeneous worker partitions, in which observations are independently and identically distributed across workers, and heterogeneous partitions, in which worker-specific covariate distributions differ. Sensitivity studies and analyses of the diabetes and Engel data illustrate the trade-offs among per-observation gradient evaluations, communication, consensus, sparsity, and prediction.

Reconciling Interpretability with Covariate-Dependent Shape Flexibility in Penalized Transformation Models for Distributional Regression

Johannes Brachem, Thomas Kneib

Quantile and distributional regressionVariational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

A central challenge in distributional regression is to allow the shape of the conditional distribution of the response variable to vary flexibly with covariates while retaining directly interpretable effects on its mean and standard deviation. We extend the penalized transformation model (PTM) family into a conditional-shape PTM, which assigns separate structured additive predictors to the conditional mean, standard deviation, and standardized distributional shape beyond location and scale. A covariate-dependent monotone transformation maps the standardized response to a fixed reference distribution, while affine standardization enforces mean zero and variance one for the induced standardized distribution. Thus, the first two predictors remain exactly the conditional mean and standard deviation. The shape predictor accommodates selected linear, nonlinear, group-specific, spatial, and interaction effects; regularization shrinks unsupported departures toward a reference-family location-scale model. We fit the PTM using mini-batched stochastic variational inference with model-aligned Gaussian blocks and staged optimization. In simulations, the PTM recovers smooth mean and standard-deviation effects and a covariate-dependent transition from skewness to bimodality while suppressing unnecessary shape effects. Under a deliberately misspecified design, it remains competitive with a structured additive Dirichlet-process mixture in test-set density and distribution-function accuracy, although all models show undercoverage and both flexible methods miss fine features. Applications to $13{,}425$ Norwegian water-conductivity observations and $1{,}182{,}514$ German daily-temperature observations demonstrate selective group-specific, seasonal, and spatial shape variation. Predictive performance criteria favor the conditional-shape PTM over a fixed-shape PTM and a Gaussian location-scale model.

cs.IT · cs.LG · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-19 · Rule-ranked

Robust Latent Variable Graphical Models: Exact Recovery and Fundamental Limits

Chuansen Peng, Xiaojing Shen

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimationHierarchical and mixed modelsOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation, Hierarchical and mixed models, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study robust estimation of a Gaussian precision matrix that decomposes as a sparse matrix minus a symmetric, possibly indefinite, low-rank matrix, a structure that arises when latent-variable marginalization is combined with conditioning on linear measurements. The proposed convex estimator replaces the sample covariance by the centered Gaussian measure-transformed (MT) covariance, which preserves the signed graph of the sparse component under the nominal model, and penalizes the low-rank component by the nuclear norm of a congruence transform, an atomic norm that encodes structural side information. Our main result is a primal--dual witness theorem: under restricted invertibility and irrepresentability conditions on the Fisher information, the raw convex optimizer is unique, has no false edges, recovers the signs of all sufficiently strong edges, and has exactly the true rank and inertia. These conditions reduce to the graphical-lasso conditions when the low-rank part is absent, and we verify them from primitive incoherence and near-isotropy assumptions. Over a class on which they hold uniformly, we prove information-theoretic limits for Frobenius estimation, signed graph recovery, and inertia recovery. In particular, inertia recovery requires $N\gtrsim p/ν_{\min}^2$ samples, where $ν_{\min}$ is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue modulus of the low-rank part; the spectral beta-min condition of the witness theorem is necessary up to a $\sqrt{\log p}$ factor; and the Frobenius risk attains the minimax rate up to a factor $\sqrt{μ(1+\min\{d,r\})\log p}$, where $μ$ is the incoherence parameter, $d$ the maximal degree, and $r$ the rank. We further quantify robustness under Huber contamination, with a bias bound that is sharp for the MT functional and vanishes for remote outliers, and give a globally convergent linearized ADMM with closed-form updates.

Nonlinear Joint State and Noise Estimation under Packet Dropouts and Additive Measurement Corruption

Peng Sun, Ruoyu Wang, Xue Luo

Variational inference and Bayesian computationNetworks and covariance estimationDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 7.0/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Networks and covariance estimation, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 2; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper investigates joint state and noise covariance estimation for nonlinear systems subject to dual reliability impairments: random packet dropouts and additive measurement corruption. This scenario poses challenging nonconjugate inference, as clean and corrupted measurements share an unknown baseline covariance $\boldsymbol{R}_k$ with fixed known corruption covariance $\boldsymbol{E}_k$. Packet reception is observable, whereas the authenticity of received measurements remains latent and must be inferred. To resolve this intractable estimation problem, we cast joint posterior inference as a variational optimization problem and develop a conjugate variational inference adaptive Kalman filter with dual reliability (CVIAKF-DR). The proposed method retains analytical updates for the prediction covariance and two reliability probabilities, while adopting CVI to estimate the state and baseline measurement covariance. The covariance update combines the analytical contribution from clean measurements with Monte Carlo gradient estimates for corrupted measurements. Numerical results on a re-entry tracking benchmark demonstrate the superior accuracy of the proposed method in both state and noise covariance estimation. Remarkably, even built upon the linear Kalman filtering (KF) framework that is originally designed for linear systems, our method achieves more accurate estimation performance than dedicated nonlinear filtering algorithms based on the cubature Kalman filter (CKF).

Locally sparse estimation for simultaneous functional quantile regression

Boyi Hu, Jiguo Cao

Abstract and links

Motivated by the study of how daily temperature affects soybean yield, this article proposes a simultaneous functional quantile regression (FQR) model featuring a locally sparse bivariate slope function indexed by both quantile and time and linked to a functional predictor. The slope function's local sparsity means it holds non-zero values only in certain segments of its domain, remaining zero elsewhere. These zero-slope regions, which vary by quantile, indicate times when the functional predictor has no discernible impact on the response variable. This feature boosts the model's interpretability. We tested the new approach through simulation studies, demonstrating its clear advantages over standard techniques. To validate its practical use, we applied the method to soybean yield data, pinpointing the time periods when daily temperature doesn't affect yield. Inference for Functional Data under Markov Constraints

Ulysse Naepels, Victor M. Panaretos

Sparsity and model selectionFunctional data and local sparsityNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 6.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Functional data and local sparsity, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Smoothness has long been the dominant form of parsimony in functional data analysis, to the point of occasionally being conflated with the very notion of functional data. However, many core inferential tasks depend on the inverse covariance, where sparsity--rather than smoothness--emerges as the more natural structural constraint. In this paper, we explore Markovianity as an alternative to smoothness. Focusing on the Gaussian case as a central motivating setting, we exploit the fact that Markovianity induces a shape constraint on the covariance kernel. Building on this observation, we introduce a Markov transform of the empirical covariance together with a corresponding estimator that enforces the Markov structure. The estimator is adaptive and requires no regularity of the underlying covariance beyond continuity. In simulation experiments, it is seen to improve prediction performance even under model misspecification. Unlike smoothness-based assumptions, Markovianity is falsifiable. To assess its validity, we further propose a novel and computationally efficient test for the Markov property based on a new characterization of continuous graphical structure.

Sparse Network Inference under Imperfect Detection and its Application to Ecological Networks

Aoran Zhang, Tianyao Wei, Maria J. Guerrero, César A. Uribe

Sparsity and model selectionHierarchical and mixed modelsOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 6.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Hierarchical and mixed models, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Recovering latent structure from count data has received considerable attention in network inference, particularly when one seeks both cross-group interactions and within-group similarity patterns in bipartite networks, which are widely used in ecology research. Such networks are often sparse and prone to imperfect detection. Existing models mainly focus on interaction recovery, while the induced similarity graphs are much less studied. Moreover, existing models for under-counted data place sparsity, if at all, on the detection model rather than on these graphs. To address these issues, we propose a nonnegative low-rank factorization framework that jointly estimates detection probabilities and promotes sparse within- and cross-group graphs through $\ell_{1/2}$ regularization. We also give sufficient conditions for exact recovery of within- and cross-group graphs. On synthetic data with up to 20% missing entries, the method recovers the graphs and detection probabilities with competitive accuracy. Across three ecological datasets, it matches the baseline held-out predictions while estimating detection probabilities and within- and cross-group graphs.

cs.LG · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-04-28 · Rule-ranked

Occam's Razor is Only as Sharp as Your ELBO

Ethan Harvey, Geoff Pleiss, Michael C. Hughes

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 6.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The marginal likelihood, also known as the evidence, is regarded as a mathematical embodiment of Occam's razor, enabling hyperparameter selection that avoids overfitting. The evidence lower bound (ELBO) objective from variational inference has also been used for similar purposes. In this paper, we define a signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) for Bayesian linear regression in terms of key hyperparameters controlling signal and noise variance. We then study how ELBO-based hyperparameter learning can under or overestimate SNR depending on the assumed covariance matrix structure in a Gaussian approximate posterior. We prove that assuming a diagonal or low-rank plus diagonal covariance matrix underestimates the SNR, while a low-rank covariance matrix overestimates the SNR. Underestimating the SNR leads to higher than optimal mean squared error on the training set (underfitting), while overestimating SNR leads to lower error (overfitting). Bayesian practitioners hoping to scale to large models should be cautious about how covariance assumptions needed for tractability may impact model selection.

High-Dimensional Multivariate VAR Estimation with Spatio-Temporal Structure

Peiliang Bai

Sparsity and model selectionDynamic and spatial modelsOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 6.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Dynamic and spatial models, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

High-dimensional vector autoregressive (VAR) models provide a flexible framework for characterizing dynamic dependence in multivariate spatio-temporal systems, but their unrestricted estimation becomes infeasible when multiple variables are observed over many spatial locations. This paper develops a structured estimation procedure for high-dimensional multivariate VAR processes that explicitly incorporates spatial information. We decompose each block transition matrix into a cross-variable dependence coefficient and a spatial transition matrix, and constrain the spatial transition matrices through a pre-specified spatial graph. The resulting estimator is formulated as a weighted $\ell_1$-regularized least-squares problem, where the weights encode spatial proximity or topological similarity and induce stronger shrinkage on spatially implausible interactions. Since the objective function is bi-convex, we estimate the cross-variable dependence matrix and the spatial transition matrices through an alternating convex-search algorithm implemented with ADMM. Under stability and restricted-eigenvalue-type conditions for high-dimensional VAR processes, we establish convergence to a blockwise stationary point in the subgradient sense and derive high-probability estimation error bounds for both components of the model. Simulation studies demonstrate that the proposed estimator accurately recovers sparse transition structures and improves over existing two-step $\ell_1$-regularized methods in support recovery and estimation accuracy. An application to North American climate data illustrates that the method recovers interpretable variable-dependence networks and spatial interaction patterns across different climate regions.
