stat.CO · Submitted 2026-08-31 · Rule-ranked

Scalable Statistical Inference in Stochastic Gradient Descent

Rahul Singh, Abhinek Shukla

Networks and covariance estimationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Constructing confidence regions for stochastic gradient descent (SGD) ideally requires estimating the asymptotic covariance matrix, a severe computational bottleneck in high dimensions. Traditional cancellation-based batch means methods bypass this estimation but require inverting a sample batch covariance matrix. This introduces strict mathematical degeneracy when the parameter dimension exceeds the number of batches. To address this problem, we utilize equal batch size batch means method and propose a simultaneous, marginal-friendly framework. The proposed marginal statistics has a asymptotic Student's $t$-distribution, and eliminates the matrix inversion step, entirely circumventing high-dimensional degeneracy. To achieve valid simultaneous coverage, we present an algorithm utilizing wild bootstrap samples drawn from a statistic as a function of only the diagonals of the variance-covariance estimator, and to further incorporate the contribution of cross-dependencies, we introduce an efficient Quasi-Monte Carlo procedure utilizing a $t$-copula approximation. Additionally, we integrate a Lugsail variance estimator to aggressively correct finite-sample bias and under-coverage. The proposed methodology delivers interpretable, simultaneous hyper-rectangular confidence regions that are statistically robust, memory-efficient, and strictly scalable for high-dimensional inference. The theoretical results are supported by extensive numerical simulation analysis through various aspects of dimension, number of batches and error structure.

math.ST · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-08-31 · Rule-ranked

Parameterising Gaussian Graphical Models

Jack Storror Carter

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Gaussian graphical models (GGMs) describe the dependence structure among jointly Gaussian random variables. However, the most common parameterisation of GGMs, the precision matrix, describes both the dependence and scale of the variables. This has been shown to lead to model selection methods that depend on the scale of the variables, despite graphical models being scale invariant. Even after standardising data to have unit sample variances, entries of the precision matrix can be on different scales leading to poor model selection. This paper decomposes the precision matrix into marginal variances and interpretable scale-invariant parameters - the partial correlations and variance inflation factors. This decomposition gives new insights into the precision matrix entries and the dynamics of model selection methods such as penalised likelihoods. In particular, it explains the observed phenomenon that penalties on the precision matrix perform poorly at selecting hub variables and motivates the necessity of data standardisation. It also shows why methods based on partial correlations have better hub detection properties. The effect of penalisation of different quantities on the estimation of marginal variances is then investigated and an interesting simplification of the log-likelihood is shown when using maximum likelihood estimation of the marginal variances.

Fast Computation of Nested Cross-Validation for Penalized Regression

Shuyang Cao, Alex Stringer

Sparsity and model selectionFunctional data and local sparsity

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Functional data and local sparsity; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Cross-validation is a resampling procedure that provides a point estimate of generalization error for any predictive model. Cross-validation is widely used for model selection and evaluation. Uncertainty in the cross-validation estimate is challenging to quantify, and estimation of its variance is known to require multiple runs of the entire resampling procedure. Nested cross-validation computes a prediction interval for the generalization error for a given model and training dataset by resampling the entire cross-validation procedure but incurs extraordinary computational cost. We provide an efficient method for computing the nested cross-validation prediction interval using only a single model fit for some penalized regression models including ridge regression, spline smoothing, and some functional regression models. We characterize when our proposed method should be expected to out-perform resampling-based nested cross-validation in various scaling regimes as well as in finite samples. Experiments for functional principle components regression demonstrate non-trivial cases in which our proposed method improves run times substantially.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-09-04 · Rule-ranked

Adaptive Regularization via Extreme Value Distributions for Gaussian Graphical Models

Alexander P. Christensen, Jeongwon Choi, Haoyi Yang, Lingzhou Xue

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Edge selection in Gaussian graphical models is fundamentally a variable selection problem where pairwise relationships determine construct validity and variable importance in psychological networks. In psychology, network estimation relies predominantly on \(\ell_1\) regularization where uniform shrinkage systematically underestimates edge and centrality parameters. Alternative penalties overcome this bias but rely on fixed hyperparameters that do not adapt to the signal in the data. We develop a family of data-adaptive regularization penalties grounded in extreme value theory. Across 290 empirical psychological datasets, we show that absolute partial correlations are well-described by the Weibull distribution. Using this empirical regularity, we derive Weibull, Gumbel, and Exponential penalties that approximate \(\ell_0\) penalization and calibrate their hyperparameters to each dataset's noise floor. We formally prove the asymptotic properties of their static forms and conduct a large-scale simulation spanning two network topologies and various sample sizes (\(N\) = 100--10,000), demonstrating that their adaptive forms maintain high specificity while accumulating sensitivity as sample size increases with low parameter bias and high rank-order centrality congruence relative to field standards. Empirically, method choice alone determined centrality rankings at sample sizes typical in psychology. Of the three adaptive penalties, Weibull is recommended given the interpretability of its parameters.

stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-09-07 · Rule-ranked

Efficient model exploration with the integrated nested Laplace approximation

Héctor López-Gómez, Virgilio Gómez-Rubio, Gonzalo García-Donato

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Model and variable selection are important topics in Bayesian inference. In particular, the selection of different fixed, random effects and hyperparameters in hierarchical models can be difficult because of their complex structure. In this paper, we introduce the use of approximate Bayesian inference for model and variable selection for hierarchical Bayesian models. The approach is based on the application of Markov chain Monte Carlo methods on the model space. In particular, the Metropolis-Hastings algorithm is used to sample from the set of model indices. In this way, the marginal likelihood is used to compute the acceptance probability so that it is not required to estimate the parameter models directly. For this, the integrated nested Laplace approximation (INLA) is used because it provides accurate estimates of the marginal likelihood and it can also provide estimates of the posterior marginal of the model parameters. This method can be applied not only to variable selection but to a wide range of problems subject to model uncertainty. To illustrate the potential of this approach, examples on variable selection, changepoint models and log-Gaussian Cox processes are developed.

cs.LG · econ.EM · stat.AP · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-11 · Rule-ranked

Beyond Point Forecasts: A Survey on Probabilistic Forecasting for Time Series and Spatiotemporal Data

Donia Besher, Rajdeep Pathak, Madhurima Panja, Tanujit Chakraborty

Quantile and distributional regressionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Probabilistic forecasting is central to decision-making under uncertainty, yet its methodological landscape has become increasingly fragmented across temporal and spatiotemporal forecasting, statistical modeling, machine learning, and deep generative modeling. This survey develops a unified perspective by organizing probabilistic forecasting methods according to where and how uncertainty is introduced into the forecasting pipeline. Our taxonomy connects model-agnostic approaches including ensembles and distribution-free calibration, with model-intrinsic approaches spanning Bayesian modeling, parametric predictive distributions, distributional regression, and modern generative models, and further examines the emerging role of time series foundation models. Beyond methodological synthesis, we identify the assumptions, computational demands, and forms of uncertainty represented by different paradigms, and translate these distinctions into data-driven and domain-specific guidance for method selection. We complement the survey with a cross-paradigm empirical study on univariate, multivariate, and spatiotemporal forecasting tasks. The results reveal that no single uncertainty-quantification paradigm dominates across settings. Calibration, sharpness, predictive accuracy, and computational efficiency can lead to substantially different model preferences, while expressive generative models and zero-shot foundation models exhibit markedly different accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. Lastly, we identify unresolved challenges surrounding uncertainty in evolving dependency structures, physics-informed predictive distributions, forecasting extreme events, handling count-valued, directional, and continuous-time series, and the development of unified software resources. Our survey provides both a conceptual framework and a practical roadmap for probabilistic forecasting research.

On Detecting Multiple Simultaneous Change-points in High Dimensional Non-Stationary Time Series

Richard Song

Sparsity and model selectionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

This paper studies the detection of multiple simultaneous (systematic) change points for high-dimensional nonstantionary economic and financial time series data. The analytic framework used is based on the standard and adaptive fused group lasso method, where the mixed L_{2,1} penalty is either uniform or re-weighted by data-dependent weights. This paper shows that, under appropriate conditions, this approach is L_2 consistent and, by adopting the data-dependent weights, could correctly select the change points with probability approaching unity (L_0 consis- tency). It quantifies the conditions on the interplay among the averaged minimum magnitude of structural changes, the number of change points and the number of observations for consistently discovering the change points. The performance of this approach is illustrated via an analysis of a large panel of U.S. economic and financial time series data over the past 50 years.

Combining Weather Forecast Aggregation and State-Space Models for Adaptive Probabilistic Electricity Load Forecasting

Joseph de Vilmarest, Jonathan Dumas, Jean Thorey

Hierarchical and mixed modelsDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Hierarchical and mixed models, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Accurate electricity load forecasting is essential to ensure the real-time balance between supply and demand, especially in systems increasingly influenced by weather conditions and renewable energy integration. In this paper, we propose an adaptive probabilistic forecasting framework that leverages multiple meteorological forecast providers to mitigate the impact of weather prediction errors on load forecasts. Our approach combines an adaptive generalized additive model (GAM) with a state-space formulation estimated via the Viking algorithm, enabling dynamic parameter updates over time. Regularized Estimation of Spatial Patterns

Wen-Ting Wang

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Global warming and the El Niño phenomenon cause various climate changes and anomalies worldwide. Recently, extreme weather events such as heatwaves, droughts, and floods have become increasingly frequent. Researchers have studied atmospheric dynamics to reduce potential damage and increase safety. Statistical methods such as principal component analysis and maximum covariance analysis have been widely used to analyze spatial patterns of atmospheric variables. However, when the signal-to-noise ratio is low, the patterns obtained from these methods are often too noisy to be physically meaningful. This dissertation proposes regularization methods that simultaneously incorporate smoothness and sparseness penalties to estimate spatial patterns more interpretably. The proposed methods, called SpatPCA and SpatMCA, can be applied to both regularly and irregularly spaced data. An efficient algorithm based on the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) is developed for computation. Through analysis of sea surface temperature data in the Indian Ocean and precipitation data in East Africa, we demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods in revealing the spatial structure and studying how temperature variations in the Indian Ocean influence precipitation in East Africa.

cs.AI · cs.LG · math.ST · physics.comp-ph · physics.data-an · Submitted 2026-09-16 · Rule-ranked

Double descent is the principle of least action

Congzhou M Sha

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The test error of a model plotted against its number of parameters $d$ falls, peaks when the model can just fit the training data, and falls again, exhibiting the double descent phenomenon. We explain the phenomenon with statistical mechanics. The training trajectory of a stochastic gradient-based method is a particle wandering over the energy landscape of the training loss at an induced temperature $T$, and a run that has equilibrated visits every parameter vector of a given training loss equally often, the fundamental postulate of statistical mechanics, with probability given by the Boltzmann distribution. Because training starts at an initial point and has only finite time to diffuse, it carries an effective weight decay, which makes every parameter a quadratic degree of freedom. The equipartition theorem then distributes the energy among the $d$ degrees of freedom in shares of $T/2$, so at a fixed training loss adding parameters lowers the temperature and drives the Boltzmann distribution toward the stationary path. Finally, adding parameters can only lower the $L^2$ norm of the stationary path, so a solution sampled at fixed loss is less likely to be large with increasing $d$, effectively increasing weight regularization.

cs.CE · cs.IT · cs.LG · math.NA · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-21 · Rule-ranked

Variational objectives for amortized Bayesian inference in inverse problems: The role of posterior conditioning

Abhishek Srivastava, Arijit Hazra, Rajesh Dubbaku

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Variational autoencoders (VAEs) offer an efficient approach to amortized Bayesian inference for inverse problems, but posterior accuracy can depend strongly on the choice of variational regularization, particularly when the inverse problem contains weakly identified parameter directions. This study investigates three objectives: a reverse Kullback--Leibler formulation (VAE-KL), an asymmetric Jensen--Shannon formulation (VAE-JS), and a Jensen--Shannon--Wasserstein formulation (VAE-JSWA), which replaces the reverse Kullback--Leibler regularizer with the squared 2-Wasserstein distance while retaining forward-Kullback--Leibler posterior supervision. A full-covariance Gaussian encoder and a pre-trained physics-based surrogate are used for amortized posterior inference. A local linear--Gaussian analysis in the generalized Fisher basis is developed to characterize the variance-dependent gradients of the three objectives. The formulations are first evaluated using linear--Gaussian benchmarks with known posterior solutions and subsequently tested on nonlinear physics-based inverse problems, including an inverse problem governed by a linear ODE and two PDE-constrained problems. VAE-KL performs slightly better than the other formulations in the well-conditioned benchmark, where all three approaches yield comparable posterior approximations, whereas VAE-JSWA provides substantially lower posterior errors in the strongly ill-conditioned benchmark. The nonlinear physics-based problems exhibit a similar conditioning-dependent trend, with JS-based formulations providing greater benefit as posterior ill-conditioning increases. These results indicate that posterior conditioning is an important factor in selecting variational objectives and motivate geometry-adaptive variational inference for Bayesian inverse problems.

math.PR · math.ST · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-09-22 · Rule-ranked

A Functional Central Limit Theorem for Locally Stationary Time Series in Banach Spaces

Florian Heinrichs, Luis-Alberto Rodríguez

Functional data and local sparsityDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Functional data and local sparsity, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We establish a functional central limit theorem for locally stationary time series in a separable Banach space $B$. The result requires no type-2 or cotype assumptions and thus covers spaces central to functional data analysis, such as $C([0,1])$ and $L^p([0,1])$. Under moment conditions, summable physical dependence coefficients and a bracketing entropy condition controlling the infinite-dimensional tails, the centered partial sum process converges weakly in $D([0,1],B)$ to a centered Gaussian process, whose covariance is the integrated local long-run covariance, an element of the projective tensor product. We also obtain a stochastic integral representation with respect to a cylindrical Brownian motion. As the limit depends on the unknown long-run covariance, we prove consistency of a block multiplier bootstrap for the partial sum process. This yields CUSUM tests for a constant mean of the full process, based on arbitrary continuous seminorms and valid under a time-varying long-run covariance. For linear projections, we derive a self-normalized test with a pivotal limit. In simulations, the supremum norm test detects localized changes that its $L^2$ counterpart largely misses, and applications to EEG and temperature data illustrate the methodology. Examples in $C([0,1])$ and $W^{1,p}_0([0,1])$ show how the entropy condition can be verified.

High-dimensional Gaussian Graphical Model Testing for Long-Memory Time Series

Percy S. Zhai, Ping-Shou Zhong, Wei Biao Wu

Networks and covariance estimationDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Many real-world high-dimensional time series exhibit long-memory, but Gaussian graphical model testing in this regime remains understudied. We develop a direct, data-adaptive test statistic for assessing conditional independence in the graph structure of stationary Gaussian time series. We establish a finite-sample, Berry--Esseen type Gaussian approximation bound for the statistic, which applies to both short-memory and long-memory time series. The testing procedure uses a block bootstrap method, on which we provide a finite-sample validity result including in the ultra-high-dimensional scenario, and can be extended to comparing graphical structures in two-sample tests. We also develop a consistency-empowered correction to the statistic and show that such tests attain asymptotic consistency in both size and power. Our proposed method is applied to real-world fMRI data to understand functional connectivities within brain in different periods.

math.ST · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-27 · Rule-ranked

Valid and Efficient Split Conformal Regression for Time Series

Percy S. Zhai, Maggie Cheng, Wei Biao Wu

Quantile and distributional regressionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study conformalized quantile regression and conformalized median regression that fit a model on one block of a time series and calibrate the conformal interval on the adjacent block. The existing theory of conformal prediction for time series rests largely on mixing conditions, which are hard to verify from a time-series model and fail for many standard processes, including simple ones with short memory. We replace this theoretical toolbox with the functional dependence measure, which in principle accommodates long-memory observations. The accuracy of the conformal interval length for time series has been understudied. To the best of our knowledge, this paper is the first work that establishes non-asymptotic coverage guarantees and accuracy of interval length simultaneously for split conformal regression on time series. Furthermore, for Gaussian linear processes with long memory, where both the estimation of the center and its calibration converge slowly, we establish a sharper rate for the length error. We show that the calibrated length converges faster than the estimated center itself, and provide a matching lower bound for the usual centers when the calibration block is sufficiently large relative to the training block. To our knowledge, this is the first theoretical analysis of conformal interval length dedicated to long memory.

eess.SP · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-28 · Rule-ranked

Nonnegative DAG Learning via Concomitant Estimation

Madeline Navarro, Gonzalo Mateos, Samuel Rey

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We study the problem of learning directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) with nonnegative edge weights from observational data. We propose the Nonnegative and Concomitant (NoCo) DAG estimator, which jointly recovers the weighted graph structure and the exogenous noise variances in the linear structural equation model for the observations. Different from prior art, this noise-adaptive formulation blends a smoothed concomitant lasso criterion with a simpler log-determinant acyclicity constraint that exploits nonnegativity and yields a more benign optimization landscape. Specifically, nonnegative weights allow us to impose acyclicity directly on the adjacency matrix without elementwise squaring of its entries, thus avoiding the well-documented degeneracy of the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions. Computationally, we develop a method of multipliers' algorithm that accommodates both homoscedastic and heteroscedastic noise profiles. Within each iteration, we use block successive convex approximation to minimize the augmented Lagrangian, alternating between proximal gradient steps for the adjacency matrix and closed-form updates for the noise scales. Simulated experiments demonstrate NoCo's improved structural and edge-weight recovery relative to competing methods in a variety of settings, highlighting the benefits of exploiting nonnegativity along with noise adaptivity.

cs.LG · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-28 · Rule-ranked

A Hierarchy of Entropy-Shapley Games for Multivariate Predictive Uncertainty

Niklas Koenen, Claudia Battistin, Jeriek Van den Abeele, Martin Jullum

Quantile and distributional regressionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Modern probabilistic machine learning models increasingly produce multivariate outputs with complex dependence structure, from multi-step time-series forecasts to sample path predictions. Understanding which input features drive the predictive uncertainty is important for risk-aware decisions, model diagnostics, and deciding whether the uncertainty should be mitigated or hedged against. This attribution problem requires a choice of how dependencies between output components are treated. Existing approaches reduce the output to a scalar through aggregation or projection before attribution, thereby obscuring whether features affect marginal uncertainty, dependence structure, or both, while component-wise analyses can miss dependence effects entirely. We close this gap by introducing a hierarchy of three entropy-based Shapley games that make this output-side choice explicit for any ordered multivariate outcome, ranging from per-component marginal entropy to fully joint entropy. The hierarchy isolates a cross-component attribution term that captures how each feature shifts the dependence between output components, a quantity invisible to component-wise methods. We establish a chain-rule decomposition of the joint attribution and characterize the cross-component term through conditional total correlation, providing both closed-form and sample-based estimators. Finally, we demonstrate how the framework captures differences in learned joint structure across probabilistic models from distributional regression to a zero-shot time series foundation model.

Tail Halos: The Covariate Dual of the Tail

Gianmarco Callegher, Miguel de Carvalho

Quantile and distributional regressionSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Extreme events are often studied through regression models describing how covariates modify tail parameters. In many applications, however, interest lies directly in identifying the parts of the covariate space associated with tail events and with elevated exceedance probabilities. We formalize these ideas through tail halos, covariate-defined regions associated with extremes. Risk halos, when they exist, are minimum-mass covariate regions accounting for a prescribed fraction of exceedance probability, while favorable halos collect covariate values where conditional exceedance probability exceeds its marginal level. To make these set-valued objects interpretable beyond low dimensions, we introduce halo-induced covariate laws and, when densities exist, corresponding halo densities. We develop a Bayesian structured additive distributional regression framework based on extended generalized Pareto marginals, covariate-dependent copulas, and spike-and-slab effect selection to learn marginal and joint halo-induced covariate laws while propagating posterior uncertainty. The approach extends Bayesian shrinkage methods for conditional tail modeling to multivariate bulk-and-tail modeling, nonlinear effect selection, and covariate-region inference. Simulations show recovery of relevant nonlinear effects and increasingly accurate finite-sample halo representations as sample size grows. An application to PM2.5 and NO2 extremes in Edinburgh reveals distinct environmental and temporal configurations associated with high pollution.

econ.EM · stat.AP · stat.ME · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-09-04 · Rule-ranked

Dynamic Spatial Bayesian Machine Learning Model: Applications to Intergenerational Economic Mobility and Geographic Income Inequality in the United States

Hammed A. Olayinka, Saheed O. Olayemi

Sparsity and model selectionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We develop a Dynamic Spatial Panel Bayesian Additive Regression Trees model with Horseshoe shrinkage (DSP-BART-HS) for high-dimensional spatio-temporal panel data. We jointly evaluate the model against a comprehensive suite of structural spatial econometrics, non-parametric machine learning methods, and small-area estimators across nine data-generating scenarios spanning 200 replicates each, including irregular spatial topologies, dense policy effects, and non-linear individual-level interactions. DSP-BART-HS is the best or statistically indistinguishable from the best estimator in every scenario. Conventional region-time-aggregate comparators suffer severe performance degradation -- trailing by a factor of three or more -- whenever individual-level non-linearity drives outcome variance, a limitation this framework's tree-ensemble design directly addresses. The model also maintains strong predictive accuracy under a zero-training-region spatial holdout via its spatial diffusion mechanism. We demonstrate practical utility across two U.S. county-level panel applications -- intergenerational economic mobility and geographic income inequality -- under genuine unseen-region, random, and temporal holdouts, with results validated through repeated-split uncertainty quantification and paired significance testing against every comparator. One clear limitation emerges: under temporal extrapolation specifically, a lighter per-region autoregressive specification (MTS-CAR-X) achieves a robust, and consistent advantage. DSP-BART-HS nonetheless establishes strong predictive performance, automated variable selection, and flexible inference for hierarchical spatio-temporal panel data.

High-Dimensional Regularization of the Spatial Sign Covariance Matrix for Robust Shape Estimation

Jonas Elmerraji, James C. Spall, Mateo Díaz

Sparsity and model selectionNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Covariance estimation is a key component of many applications in system identification and data-driven control. Although heavy-tailed distributions may lack a covariance matrix to estimate, the shape matrix provides a well-defined, scale-free generalization for the broad family of elliptical distributions. In this setting, practitioners often employ Tyler's M-estimator (TME), which is defined implicitly and is computed using a fixed-point iteration. The spatial sign covariance matrix estimator (SSCM) offers a much simpler alternative: it corresponds to one iteration of TME. Yet, the SSCM has been largely regarded as an inferior estimator due to its statistical inconsistency under fixed-dimensional asymptotics. By contrast, using second-order tools from random matrix theory, we establish that, under standard assumptions, SSCM asymptotically dominates TME in Frobenius risk when dimension and sample size grow proportionally. This advantage arises from implicit regularization, which reduces variance at the cost of a bias that vanishes as the dimension grows. Numerical experiments support the theoretical predictions even at moderate dimensions.

Amortized Structured Stochastic Variational Inference for Gaussian Process Latent Variable Models

Maksym Tretiakov, Sarah Filippi, Vincent Fortuin, Ruth Misener, Ruby Sedgwick, James Odgers

Variational inference and Bayesian computationHierarchical and mixed models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Many machine learning methods aim to approximate the lower-dimensional manifold on which the data lives. A desirable feature of such methods is that they should capture the epistemic uncertainty of this learned manifold. One model that achieves this is the Gaussian Process Latent Variable Model, in which a Gaussian Process (GP) mapping from the latent space provides an estimate of the uncertainty of the manifold. However, the effectiveness of this uncertainty estimation is limited by the mean-field variational approximation between the GP inducing points and the latent variables. In this work, we apply Amortized Structured Stochastic Variational Inference to allow the variational posterior for the latent space to be conditionally dependent on the value of the inducing points. We demonstrate that this more flexible variational posterior improves several metrics relating to the reconstruction of points on the data manifold.

cs.CR · stat.CO · Submitted 2026-10-03 · Rule-ranked

Fully Homomorphic Encryption for Statistical Modeling

Balasubramanian Narasimhan

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Fully homomorphic encryption allows arithmetic to be carried out on encrypted values, so that a party performing a computation need not see the data it operates on. This is useful wherever collaborating parties or sites cannot share data records or computed summaries yet need to do a joint analysis. We demonstrate the protocols needed to perform such analyses reproducibly via two packages in R, a platform widely used for applied statistics. The first, openfhe.R, is an interface to the OpenFHE C++ library, which exposes exact integer arithmetic (BFV, BGV), approximate real-valued arithmetic (CKKS), Boolean circuits, and multiparty key generation. The second is homomorpheR, which builds a small set of master/worker primitives for multi-party protocols on top of the first. Together, the two let an ordinary R statistical routine compose with an encrypted-arithmetic layer at the function-value boundary, provided the quantity crossing that boundary decomposes as a sum over sites. Two validation studies are presented: distributed stratified Cox regression, where stats4::mle() converges through an encrypted channel, and federated Cox-lasso via convex optimization using consensus ADMM, where the cross-site update is the only encrypted step. We also discuss federated similarity retrieval and two-party prediction. Throughout, the parties are assumed honest-but-curious, and threshold key generation removes the assumption that any single party holds a usable secret key.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-10-03 · Rule-ranked

Estimation and inference in penalized function-on-function regression with within-curve residual dependence

Fabian Scheipl

Sparsity and model selectionFunctional data and local sparsity

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Functional data and local sparsity; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Penalized function-on-function regression, as implemented in the R package refund, considers all grid values as independent conditional on the linear predictor, but some dependence typically remains. We study the consequences for estimates and pointwise intervals in a simulation and a plasmode study on nine real datasets. Under dependence, restricted maximum likelihood (REML) undersmooths the coefficient surface: in our simulations' main settings, its relative error is two to four times that of curve-blocked neighborhood cross-validation (NCV). In our plasmode study, both fits have large errors, possibly through weak identification, and NCV is usually, but not always, better. Under dependence, the independence-model-based intervals for means and surfaces undercover severely. We propose and evaluate a curve-clustered sandwich covariance with a leverage adjustment for the penalized hat matrix with Satterthwaite degrees of freedom which brings coverage close to nominal in most settings. Its intervals for function-on-function effects are wide because REML's estimate is noisy. The same construction at the NCV estimate is narrow but undercovers. We therefore recommend fitting twice: NCV for the shape of the surface, REML with these intervals for inference. For estimated means, intercept functions and scalar covariate effects, one REML fit with these intervals usually suffices.

math.ST · stat.CO · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-10-04 · Rule-ranked

A Bayesian One-Sample Test for the Mean of Functional Data

José R. Berrendero, Antonio Coín, Antonio Cuevas

Variational inference and Bayesian computationFunctional data and local sparsity

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Functional data and local sparsity; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In the context of functional data analysis, we propose a Bayesian test to assess whether the mean function of a Gaussian population is identically zero. By leveraging the structure of the reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) associated with the covariance function of the underlying process, we approximate the mean by a finite linear combination of kernel sections. This finite-dimensional representation arises from the natural assumption that the mean lies in the corresponding RKHS, enabling a parametric Bayesian approach to hypothesis testing through a spike-and-slab prior on the coefficients. Given the absence of a canonical reference measure on infinite-dimensional spaces, the likelihood is expressed via a Radon-Nikodym derivative between induced Gaussian measures. Since the number of kernel sections is unknown, we employ reversible jump Markov chain Monte Carlo (RJMCMC) to explore the trans-dimensional parameter space and estimate the posterior distribution. From the resulting posterior samples we construct estimators of the posterior probability of the null hypothesis and the Bayes factor, providing quantifiable evidence against the null and supporting informed decisions under uncertainty. Furthermore, to validate this methodology we establish near-optimal posterior contraction rates for the proposed test under mild conditions, demonstrate its empirical performance on simulated data sets, and illustrate its application with real climate data.

cs.LG · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-05 · Rule-ranked

LinearPFN: Amortized Variable Selection for Linear Models with Interactions

Louis Schiekiera, Max Zimmer, Christophe Roux, Manuel Arnold, Sebastian Pokutta, Fritz Günther

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Spike-and-slab regression is a standard Bayesian formulation of variable selection: it returns a posterior distribution over which candidate effects are active rather than a single selected subset, so that every candidate effect carries an inclusion probability. Its cost grows exponentially with the number of candidate effects, so the posterior can be enumerated exactly only when the number of predictors is small. Beyond that reach, the posterior has to be approximated, typically by Markov chain Monte Carlo over the model space, which requires a fresh run for every dataset and, within a fixed budget of steps, may fail to converge. We present LinearPFN, a prior-data fitted transformer network that amortizes spike-and-slab inference for linear models with main effects and pairwise interactions. The network is pretrained once on synthetic datasets, drawn from an explicitly specified prior, and a single forward pass over a new dataset returns posterior inclusion probabilities, posterior-mean coefficients and posterior predictive distributions with no per-dataset fitting. The prior is conjugate by design, so that the posterior for each fixed set of active effects has a closed form, and wherever the exact posterior is still computable by enumeration we verify the network's outputs against it. On real predictor matrices from published social-science datasets, with outcomes drawn from the prior so that the true active set is known, LinearPFN attains a higher per-dataset selection AUC and a higher F1 under the median probability model rule than five classical baselines. The lead holds when the coefficients, the interactions or the noise depart from the prior. Code: https://github.com/schiekiera/LinearPFN. Trained model: https://huggingface.co/schiekiera/LinearPFN.

cs.LG · stat.AP · stat.ME · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-06 · Rule-ranked

Trustworthy Method Comparison with AI Judges: Estimation and Design under Order, Batch, and Aggregation Effects

Tianxi Li, Jie Ding

Networks and covariance estimationHierarchical and mixed models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judges for automated AI evaluation. A common practice is to randomize prompt sequences and average the resulting scores, but its statistical validity remains unclear. We show that LLM evaluation mechanisms can be approximated by a class of Markov generalized linear mixed models (GLMMs), supported by out-of-sample predictions across three major commercial LLMs. Using a first-order Markov GLMM, we study leaderboard ranking and group comparison. For leaderboard ranking, randomize-and-average selection is consistent under a mild separation condition, and a Williams square design can improve efficiency when item qualities are close. For group comparison, naive averaging can yield inconsistent conclusions about differences in group-level quality because of the response model's nonlinearity. Empirical results further support the validity of the proposed model-based inference beyond the first-order theory, including settings with higher-order sequence memory. We illustrate the approach in an application where AI judges compare two graphical model estimation methods.

GPU-accelerated Regularized Vector Multiplicative Error Models for Positive-valued Financial Time Series

Rohan Hemant Chhatre, Chiranjit Dutta, Nalini Ravishanker, Sumanta Basu

Sparsity and model selectionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

The logarithmic vector multiplicative error model (log-vMEM) provides a methodology for modeling and forecasting multivariate positive-valued time series. However, the number of autoregressive parameters increases rapidly with the system dimension and lag order, posing challenges for estimation and model selection. This paper develops a regularized estimation framework for the log-vMEM with multivariate Gamma errors by incorporating hierarchical lag structures to induce sparsity and accommodate heterogeneous lag dynamics. We consider three hierarchical lag structures: componentwise, own-other, and elementwise, in combination with group lasso, adaptive group lasso, group MCP, and group SCAD penalties. Parameter estimation is carried out through an alternating block-coordinate optimization algorithm with sequential Gauss-Seidel updates, while GPU-accelerated numerical quadrature is employed to address the computational burden of evaluating the multivariate Gamma likelihood. Simulation experiments investigate parameter recovery and lag selection under different sparsity structures. The real data analysis is carried out on the realized volatility measures for Microsoft (NASDAQ:MSFT) which allows a comparison of the competing regularization approaches in an empirical financial setting. Together, these developments provide a computational framework for sparse multivariate volatility modeling with flexible lag structures and dependent Gamma errors.

cs.LG · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-06 · Rule-ranked

The Impact of Likelihood Tempering on the Limiting Predictive Moments of Variational Bayesian Linear Neural Networks

Ian Zhang, Thibault Randrianarisoa

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In wide Bayesian neural networks, Gaussian mean-field variational inference is prone to "prior dominance": the Kullback-Leibler (KL) regularization term of the ELBO outweighs the expected log-likelihood, and the variational predictive distribution collapses to the prior predictive as the width $M$ grows. Tempering the likelihood, by raising it to the power $1/T$ for a temperature $T < 1$, is equivalent to scaling the KL term by $T$. We ask in this paper how fast $T$ must decrease with $M$ to counteract this degeneracy and strike a good balance between the two terms. For single-hidden-layer linear networks with isotropic Gaussian priors, we derive the limiting predictive distribution under schedules of the form $T = τ/M^{c}$, with constants $τ, c > 0$, as $M \to \infty$ and compare it with the untempered neural network Gaussian process (NNGP) posterior, the infinite-width limit of the exact posterior. Our main result is that the predictive expectation and variance undergo phase transitions at different scales: the limiting expectation leaves its prior value at $c = 1/2$, once $τ$ falls below an explicit threshold, and equals the least-squares prediction for $c > 1/2$, whereas the limiting variance keeps its prior value for $c < 1$, matches the NNGP's for $c=1$, and vanishes for $c > 1$. With suitable choices of $τ,c$, one can recover either the NNGP posterior expectation or its variance.

Scalable Sparse Autoregressive Variational Inference for Non-Gaussian Spatial Fields

Paul F. V. Wiemann, Matthias Katzfuss

Abstract and links

Latent field models are essential for spatial statistics, but the posterior distribution of the latent field, even with a Gaussian process (GP) prior, is analytically intractable whenever the likelihood is non-Gaussian. Existing approximations are either too slow or too restrictive for large non-Gaussian datasets. Global Inference for High Dimensional Quantile Regression with Heterogeneity

Tate Jacobson, Daeyoung Ham, Ben Sherwood

Quantile and distributional regressionSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Quantile regression has received considerable attention in high dimensional methodology due to its robustness and ability to accommodate heteroskedasticity. Despite this attention, current inferential methods for high dimensional quantile regression have significant limitations---only testing significance, testing at a single quantile at a time, or assuming homoskedasticity. To generalize the inferences that can be drawn in high dimensional quantile regression, we introduce partial penalized Wald and rank score tests for testing linear hypotheses uniformly over a set of quantiles $\mathcal T$ under a heteroskedastic model. We propose globally adaptive local linear approximation (LLA) algorithms for folded concave penalized estimation and show that our global LLA algorithms recover ``testing oracles'' for the full and reduced models with high probability. We then show that our global LLA estimators achieve the $\sqrt{s/n}$ oracle estimation rate uniformly over $\mathcal T$, establish Bahadur representations, and derive asymptotic distributions for the test statistics under the null and local alternatives. We develop a dual formulation incorporating adaptive penalty weights for the rank scores and use the strong oracle property to establish their oracle equivalence. Using empirical process techniques, we derive reference distributions based on suprema of squared Bessel processes, extending partial penalized inference beyond pointwise chi-square calibration.

Two For The Price Of One: Curvature-Aware Pruning Preserves Influence Functions Too

Mehdi Makni, Natalia Ponomareva, Rahul Mazumder

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Post-training pruning enables efficient deployment, but conventional methods do not explicitly preserve internal-gradient alignment between sparse and dense models. We study novel connections between downstream performance of compressed models and the ability to retain gradient information via influence-function preservation. Central to this connection is a curvature-aware objective that accounts for both layer activations and backpropagated gradients. Despite its promise, this objective remains underexplored in pruning due to a lack of efficient solvers. We introduce $\texttt{CACTUS}$, a novel ADMM-based optimizer supporting both pruning and sparse-plus-low-rank decompositions of pre-trained weights. Compared to LLM-Surgeon which targets the same objective, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ can achieve over an order-of-magnitude speedup. At $50\%$ pruning of Qwen3-4B, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ reduces the C4 perplexity gap to the dense model by $45\%$, improves compressed-to-dense per-sample gradient cosine similarity on WikiText-2 from $0.670$ to $0.755$, and runs over $28\times$ faster than LLM-Surgeon on substantially weaker hardware. By preserving influence functions, $\texttt{CACTUS}$ supports effective data selection. On Llama-3.2-3B, fine-tuning a fresh dense model on the $5\%$ of data selected by $\texttt{CACTUS}$ achieves $49.44\%$ accuracy on BIG-Bench Hard, compared with $47.22\%$ using ALPS and $49.54\%$ using dense-model selection. Finally, integrating $\texttt{CACTUS}$ with LoGRA, a recent gradient-projection strategy, on Qwen3-4B with MLP layers pruned to 2:4 sparsity and a rank-64 correction retains a $0.711$ cosine alignment with dense LoGRA-projected C4 gradients.

stat.ME · Submitted 2026-10-16 · Rule-ranked

Joint Estimation and Feature Selection in Overspecified Finite Mixture of Regressions

Abbas Khalili, Pengqi Liu, Huy Nguyen, Nhat Ho

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We investigate joint estimation and feature selection in sparse finite mixtures of regressions with an overspecified number of mixture components or mixture order. More specifically, given a conservative fixed upper bound on the unknown mixture order, we deliberately overspecify the order and propose a double-penalized likelihood approach for simultaneous parameter estimation and feature selection. We establish consistent recovery of the sparsity structure of the underlying mixing measure in terms of its regression coefficients, showing that the true sparsity of the mixture regression can be consistently identified even under order over-specification. The proposed methodology incorporates two complementary penalties: the first penalizes small (near-zero) mixing proportions, inducing an asymptotic Voronoi-type partition of the estimated regression vectors into clusters associated with the true sparse regression vectors; the second penalizes regression coefficients, resulting in sparsity through the selection of relevant features. Although the sparsity penalty is applied to individual regression coefficients, the combined effect of the two penalties yields an asymptotic group feature selection property where the same features tend to be selected or not selected within each Voronoi cluster. We develop a modified EM algorithm combined with proximal gradient for numerical implementations. Extensive simulations and a real data application demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

cs.AI · cs.LG · econ.EM · stat.ME · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-16 · Rule-ranked

Graph-Regularized Synthetic Control for Stable Counterfactual Estimation under Donor Redundancy

Yanlin Zhang, Sungyong Chung, Alireza Talebpour

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Synthetic control methods estimate counterfactual outcomes by weighting untreated donor units to match a treated unit before treatment. In recent applications, however, the donor pool can be large, highly correlated, and short in pre-treatment history, yielding many near-equivalent solutions with unstable and difficult-to-interpret donor weights. We propose Graph-Regularized Synthetic Control (Graph-SC), a convex estimator that augments the classical pre-treatment matching objective with a graph Laplacian penalty. The graph encodes similarity among donor trajectories, encouraging coherent weights over structurally related donors while preserving the transparency of synthetic controls. We provide theoretical results characterizing the bias-variance tradeoff induced by graph regularization and establish efficient convergence guarantees with an accelerated proximal gradient method. Across a controlled simulation under donor redundancy, the California Proposition 99 benchmark, and observational counterfactual analyses on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, Graph-SC improves weight stability, reduces sensitivity to redundant donor pools, and maintains competitive pre- and post-treatment accuracy relative to classical and regularized synthetic control baselines. Our results suggest that graph regularization is most valuable not as a universal accuracy booster, but as a principled mechanism for selecting stable and interpretable counterfactuals from near-equivalent synthetic controls with comparable pre-treatment fit.

Denoising Implicit Variational Inference

Zitong Wang, Zekun Wu, Longlin Yu, Cheng Zhang

Variational inference and Bayesian computationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Semi-implicit variational inference (SIVI) expands the representational capacity of variational families, but optimizing the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) remains challenging because the marginal score of the semi-implicit distribution is generally intractable. Unbiased Implicit Variational Inference (UIVI) and related methods address this difficulty through reparameterized ELBO gradients, but existing approaches estimate the required score indirectly through reverse-conditional sampling or approximation, which can be computationally costly and unstable. We propose Denoising Implicit Variational Inference (DIVI), which learns the marginal score directly via denoising score matching. The learned score is used as a plug-in estimator in the pathwise ELBO gradient, replacing inner reverse-conditional sampling with a score-network evaluation. We analyze DIVI as inexact stochastic gradient ascent and show, under stated assumptions, that the averaged stationarity measure is controlled by the usual optimization term and the average score-matching error. Empirically, we evaluate DIVI on both synthetic distributions and a variety of real-data Bayesian inference tasks. The results show that DIVI improves over the current UIVI baselines while reducing the cost of ELBO-gradient estimation.

VampPrior Unleashed

Andrew A. Stirn, Abhejit Rajagopal, David A. Knowles, Grace Huynh

Variational inference and Bayesian computationHierarchical and mixed models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Hierarchical and mixed models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Deep latent variable models (DLVMs) underpin many modern analysis pipelines, for example in single-cell genomics, yet their prior distributions have received markedly little attention. The VampPrior approximates the optimal prior (the aggregate posterior) and often attains high model evidence. Here, we identify two key limitations of the VampPrior: it ties prior and posterior uncertainty to a single amortized covariance and clusters poorly due to its fixed uniform mixture weights. We unleash the VampPrior by decoupling its prior and posterior covariances, resolving the conflict of generating high prior and low posterior entropy with the same shared parameters and substantially improving model evidence. We then replace the uniform mixture with a Dirichlet process. When combined, our proposals provide higher model evidence than the VampPrior and robust non-parametric clustering. Augmenting single cell Variational Inference (scVI) with our prior improves single-cell integration and clustering across six atlases while significantly raising model evidence. Our approach changes only the prior and thus can be applied to any DLVM with a continuous latent space.

Robust and Sparse Personalized Federated Learning via Gamma-Divergence

Kotaro Akimaru, Kensuke Tanioka

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

To address the challenges of statistical heterogeneity across clients and within-client data contamination in federated learning, we propose a robust and sparse personalized federated learning method based on the gamma-divergence, termed Gamma-PFL. Specifically, we combine a loss function based on the gamma-divergence with fusion and sparse regularization terms to exploit similarities in regression coefficients across clients while enabling robust estimation of personalized models and variable selection. Furthermore, to efficiently solve the estimation problem in the federated server--client system, we develop an optimization algorithm that combines the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers with the Majorization--Minimization algorithm. Numerical simulations showed that Gamma-PFL maintained relatively stable estimation accuracy as the contamination proportion or outlier magnitude increased, demonstrating its robustness to within-client data contamination. In addition, a real data analysis showed that Gamma-PFL achieved predictive performance comparable to or better than that of an existing personalized federated learning method.

Fast Calibration of Generalized Posteriors via Dirichlet Reweighting

Shonosuke Sugasawa

Quantile and distributional regressionSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

While generalized Bayesian inference offers a principled approach to uncertainty quantification under general loss functions, its practical performance depends critically on the choice of the learning rate. Generalized posterior calibration, which selects the learning rate so that the resulting credible sets attain nominal frequentist coverage, is computationally intensive because it relies on repeated posterior sampling across bootstrap datasets and candidate learning rates. To overcome this practical difficulty, we propose a fast calibration method that combines Dirichlet-weighted bootstrap perturbations with importance sampling to estimate coverage while reducing the need for repeated posterior sampling. We further extend the framework to hierarchical models, where group-specific learning rates are calibrated and stabilized via shrinkage. Illustrative examples using quantile regression, support vector machines, and hierarchical Huber regression demonstrate that the proposed method substantially reduces computational cost while maintaining accurate calibration.

Robust Log-Contrast Regression for High-Dimensional Compositional Microbiome Data with Outliers

Xuke Hua, Yunhai Xiao, Xin Xin

Sparsity and model selectionOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

It is well known that microbiome sequencing data are naturally compositional and often have a complex subcomposition structure, as well as a lot of noise and some outliers. These characteristics make classical log-contrast regression difficult to apply, because it is sensitive to extreme values and cannot fully meet the linear constraints of compositional predictors. To tackle these issues, in this paper, we propose a robust log-contrast regression framework that incorporates the Huber loss function along with a Lasso penalty, which not only boosts resistance to outliers but also facilitates sparse variable selection. It is important to note that the general linear constraints, which are explicitly imposed, serve to preserve the algebraic coherence of compositional and subcompositional covariates. Under some mild assumptions, we theoretically prove that the proposed method achieves non-asymptotic guarantees in finite samples, comparable to those of Huber loss estimators, ensuring robustness. To implement the method efficiently, we develop a symmetric Gauss--Seidel-based alternating direction method of multipliers based on its dual formulation with a convergence guarantee, and it is also easy to implement in the sense that each subproblem admits a closed-form solution. To evaluate the statistical performance of the proposed method, we conduct a series of numerical experiments using simulated data and gut microbiome data, which show that the proposed method achieves better estimation accuracy, competitive prediction performance, and more reliable outlier identification than the benchmark method RobRegCC.

cs.AI · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-10-27 · Rule-ranked

Rènyi-Regularized Flow-Based Variational Inference for Bayesian Calibration

Exaucè Luweh Adjim Ngarti, Élise Arnaud, Léo Nicoletti, Victor Trappler, Arthur Vidard

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Variational inference with normalizing flows provides a flexible posterior approximation for Bayesian model calibration, but struggles when the posterior has several modes separated by regions of low probability mass. We study this behavior in a two-parameter twin experiment and regularize the variational objective. Under Gaussian observation errors and a Gaussian prior, the corresponding minimization objective is the expected Tikhonov calibration objective minus the differential entropy of the approximating distribution. However, the expected Tikhonov term favors concentration near one of the modes, while entropy maximization does not guarantee multimodality. We introduce a regularization term based on Rényi divergence that encourages the approximate posterior to cover the prior support. In the connected bimodal case, a flow approximation represents both modes; in the case with well-separated modes, the flow and Gaussian mixture frequently miss one mode. The introduced regularization reduces the near-collapse fraction from 50% to 25%. While the regularization improves mode coverage in this experiment, it changes the Bayesian variational objective, introduces a trade-off in approximation accuracy, and does not provide a general solution to mode collapse.

Estimation and Inference for One-pass Streaming Function-on-Function Regression

Shuyang Jiang, Xin Liu, Peijun Sang

Functional data and local sparsityOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Functional data and local sparsity, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

In many applications, functional pairs are collected sequentially from a data stream. Existing function-on-function regression methods are primarily designed for batch data, and bootstrap inference has become one of the most popular approaches. However, this procedure may be infeasible when observations cannot be stored. In this paper, we propose a one-pass functional SGD estimator that updates upon each observation's arrival without data storage, and a weighted rolling validation rule is provided for step size selection for implementation. Further, an online multiplier bootstrap is developed for inference that updates perturbed stochastic gradient paths in parallel, bypassing estimating an infinite-dimensional covariance operator. Asymptotic behaviors are established for the proposed method, and conditional bootstrap validity is justified for the bias-centered pointwise estimation error. Simulation studies and two real data applications confirm the empirical effectiveness of the proposed procedures. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretically justified one-pass framework for bootstrap inference on a bivariate coefficient surface with streaming functional data.

Efficient Deep Gaussian Process Surrogates via Mini-Batch Latent MAP Estimation

Chun-Yi Chang, Chih-Li Sung

Variational inference and Bayesian computationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Deep Gaussian processes (DGPs) are flexible surrogate models for computer experiments with nonstationary response surfaces, but inference over their latent representations remains computationally challenging. Existing methods typically rely on variational inference or Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), which impose variational-family assumptions or require costly posterior sampling. We propose the latent maximum-a-posteriori deep Gaussian process (LaMP-DGP), which uses gradient-based optimization to obtain MAP estimates of the latent representations and enables efficient probabilistic prediction through linked-GP calculations. To scale LaMP-DGP to large datasets, we develop a mini-batch optimization algorithm and, under a polynomial mini-batch bias-decay condition, establish a stationarity bound for latent-state optimization that motivates a growing batch-size schedule. Across numerical experiments and real-data analyses, LaMP-DGP provides consistently competitive predictive performance relative to doubly stochastic variational inference, stochastic gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, and fully Bayesian Vecchia DGP inference. Computationally, LaMP-DGP is substantially faster than the Vecchia MCMC approach while remaining broadly competitive with the stochastic-gradient alternatives. These results position LaMP-DGP as a competitive and computationally efficient alternative for DGP inference. An R package, "LaMPDGP", is available for implementing the proposed method.

Variational Bayes for Variable Selection with Adaptive Sharing across Populations and Responses

Yuehan Yang, Pengfei Chen, Fan Yang, Chong You

Variational inference and Bayesian computationSparsity and model selection

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We propose a variational Bayesian framework for variable selection with multiple responses across heterogeneous populations. The prior dependence among the inclusion indicators allows variable-selection information to be shared at different levels across populations and responses, while regression coefficients remain population- and response-specific. When the appropriate sharing structure is unknown, a data-driven rule adaptively determines the scope of information sharing from marginal association evidence. The resulting variational algorithm is computationally scalable and can be implemented using either individual-level or summary data. Under regularity conditions, we establish selection consistency and convergence properties of the variational parameters at fixed iterations of the algorithm for fixed dimension case. In high dimensions, we establish model-selection consistency and coefficient contraction in both $\ell_1$ and $\ell_2$ norms. Simulation studies show competitive variable selection performance and demonstrate the potential gains from joint analysis. We further illustrate the summary-data implementation using genome-wide association summary statistics from the 2021 Global Lipids Genetics Consortium study for four lipid traits across five populations.

q-bio.PE · stat.CO · Submitted 2026-11-02 · Rule-ranked

BEAGLE in Action: Phylogenetic Likelihoods for Very Large State Spaces

Benjamin D Redelings, April Wright, Karthik Gangavarapu, Yucai Shao, Filippo Monti, Wu-Min Deng, Shuo Su, Andrew Rambaut, Guy Baele, Philippe Lemey, Marc A. Suchard, Xiang Ji

Sparsity and model selectionDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Sparsity and model selection, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Computing phylogenetic likelihoods becomes challenging when the continuous-time Markov chain has a large state space. At each branch, pruning algorithms require terms of the form $e^{Q t}\cdot B$, where $Q$ is an $S \times S$ rate matrix (e.g., for $S$ states), $t$ is a branch length, and the columns of matrix $B$ (e.g., $B \in \mathbb{R}_{+}^{S \times L}$) are partial-likelihood vectors for $L$ site patterns. Many implementations calculate the dense transition matrix $P(t)=e^{Q t}$ via eigendecomposition and multiply it by $B$. This strategy is efficient for small state spaces, but it can be inefficient for large sparse rate matrices because forming $P(t)$ or its eigen vector matrices destroys sparsity and its cost may not be well amortized when $L$ is small. We implement an alternative likelihood kernel based on the Al-Mohy and Higham (2011) algorithm for computing the action of the matrix exponential directly, avoiding explicit construction of $P(t)$ or its eigen vector matrices. The resulting method takes full advantage of the sparsity of $Q$. We implement this method as a CPU-based extension to the BEAGLE library and evaluate it on two large-state molecular models: a Markov-modulated codon model of heterotachy with $M$ selective regimes and $61\cdot M$ possible states, and a codon-pair model of interlocus gene conversion between two paralogs with $61^2=3\,721$ possible states. Our results characterize when action-based likelihood computation improves performance over calculating $e^{Q t}$ directly. We show that this method enables fast, scalable inference for complex models, removing a key computational barrier to the study of evolution with large state space models.

econ.EM · q-fin.ST · stat.ML · Submitted 2026-11-02 · Rule-ranked

PanelGP: A Probabilistic Factor Model for Asset-Return Panels

Andrea Ruglioni, Damir Filipovic

Variational inference and Bayesian computationNetworks and covariance estimation

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Networks and covariance estimation; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

We develop PanelGP, a conditional factor model for large panels of stock returns in which firm characteristics determine the factor loadings and economic conditions determine the expected factors. Both relations are estimated nonparametrically with Gaussian-process priors. The kernel choice nests instrumented principal component analysis (IPCA) as well as smooth nonlinear maps. Asset-specific loading innovations and common factor innovations separate idiosyncratic from systematic risk, so one estimated model forecasts both expected returns and their covariance matrix. Estimation uses sparse variational inference with a joint posterior over loadings and factors. Because each predicted return is bilinear in a Gaussian state, the predictive covariance matrix and the cumulants of any portfolio have closed forms. In simulations, the estimator recovers the nonlinear maps and the variance parameters. For S&P 500 constituents over 1995-2024, nonlinear loadings rank stocks better than affine loadings and improve the Gaussian log score. Relative to IPCA, the resulting portfolios have higher Sharpe ratios, also net of trading costs, and the minimum-variance portfolio has lower volatility. The predicted variances rank stocks and months by risk but are too compressed.

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Quantile and distributional regression, Sparsity and model selection; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Distributed statistical learning has recently attracted significant attention owing to its computational efficiency and scalability in large-scale systems. Last-Iterate Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Methods for Unbounded Problems

Kevin Kurian Thomas Vaidyan, Ahmet Alacaoglu, Michael P. Friedlander

Variational inference and Bayesian computationOptimisation for statistical learning

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Variational inference and Bayesian computation, Optimisation for statistical learning; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Last-iterate convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods have attracted significant attention as practical implementations typically return the last iterate instead of an average of the iterates. In this paper, we study two approaches for analyzing the last-iterate convergence of proximal and projected stochastic gradient methods without bounded domain or bounded variance assumptions, where black-box variational inference serves as a running example. Under two related assumptions relaxing the bounded variance, we show near-optimal anytime rate $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{T})$ for the last iterate. Advection-aligned block Vecchia inference for spatio-temporal Gaussian processes

Joonwon Lee, Michael L. Stein

Networks and covariance estimationDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Vecchia approximations enable scalable likelihood-based inference for large Gaussian process models, but their accuracy depends on the choice of conditioning sets. For spatio-temporal processes with advection, target-centered conditioning can omit informative past observations. Motivated by total column ozone observations from the Geostationary Environment Monitoring Spectrometer (GEMS), we propose a data-driven, advection-aligned block Vecchia approximation that incorporates estimated transport direction into temporal conditioning geometry. Observation locations need not lie on a regular grid. An initial advection vector is estimated from the data and used once to select transport-aligned past-time conditioning blocks. The resulting graph is held fixed during optimization, while covariance and advection parameters remain free to vary. This separates neighbor selection from covariance estimation, avoids repeated parameter-dependent neighbor searches, and provides an interpretable connection between conditioning geometry and estimated transport. Batched graphics processing unit (GPU) operations evaluate the likelihood and its gradients. In a matched comparison of daily GEMS datasets from July 2024 and July 2025, advection-aligned conditioning attained a lower fitted Vecchia negative log-likelihood than target-centered conditioning on 53 of 61 days. In six Matérn simulation scenarios with 120 matched eight-hour fields each, advection-aligned conditioning reduced advection-vector root mean squared error relative to target-centered conditioning at the same conditioning-block budget, with the largest reduction under strong transport.

econ.EM · stat.AP · stat.ME · Submitted 2026-11-09 · Rule-ranked

Bayesian precision factor models for high-dimensional macroeconomic time series

Peter Dunson

Networks and covariance estimationDynamic and spatial models

Relevance 4.5/10. Matched topics: Networks and covariance estimation, Dynamic and spatial models; keyword matches: 1; preferred authors: 0.

Abstract and links

Series in a macroeconomic panel are almost all correlated with one another, which makes correlation a poor guide to which pairs are directly linked. For example, Treasury rates at different maturities are all highly correlated, yet a short and a long rate may be related only through the rates at maturities between them; similarly, sectors of the economy such as output, employment, prices, and interest rates may be related directly or only through other series. Both are questions about the inverse covariance matrix. Recovering it from quarterly data is difficult: contemporaneous dependence is entangled with temporal dynamics, the innovations remain densely dependent once those dynamics are removed, and the number of series is comparable to the number of quarters observed. We propose VAPOR (Vector Autoregressive Precision factOR), a Bayesian model that estimates vector autoregressive dynamics jointly with a factor-structured innovation precision matrix, inferring the partial correlations directly from the fitted precision rather than imposing a zero pattern. Applied to 100 quarterly FRED-QD series, VAPOR learns from the data that each Treasury rate is directly related only to the rates at adjacent maturities, places all 26 selected dependencies within a single FRED-QD group, and attains the lowest held-out prediction error among the estimators compared.

Abstract and links

Over recent decades, high temperatures have intensified worldwide, with impacts on human health, ecosystems, and the economy.
